# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

## Climate Change Problem

Barbados is experiencing "absolute water scarcity" based on the Falkemir water stress index since approximately 205-310 m³ of water is available per person per year, placing Barbados amongst the most water-scarce countries in the world.

The issue of water scarcity becomes of greater concern in the face of climate change. The projections, representative for Barbados, using a multi-model CMIP ensemble with the SSP2-4.5 scenario and for the respective 1980–2000 baseline period are as follows:

* **The mean annual daily maximum temperature will increase by about 1.0°C.** The mean annual daily minimum temperature is projected to increase by approximately 0.7°C.
* **The wet season (September to November) will become drier and the dry season (December to August) will become wetter.**
* **The number of days with maximum temperatures exceeding the average daily maximum temperature at the 90th percentile has increased over the period 1991–2005 and may be expected to increase further with increasing temperatures.**

Annual rainfall is projected to decrease by approximately 3% in Barbados, particularly severe in the May – July season, effectively extending the dry season by two months.

There is a cause-and-effect relationship with each of these scenarios. For example, temperature will affect the seasonal distribution and intensity of precipitation, which will place stress on the population, including tourists and livestock, increase cooling costs, induce changes in plant-leaf-insect populations and disease vectors, and reduce crop yields. The projected increase in sea surface temperatures under climate change and SLR will promote flash flooding; saline intrusion into available ground water abstraction and storage facilities; loss of agricultural lands; coastal erosion and damage to coastal infrastructure and disruption to businesses.

The effects of climate change are already evident in Barbados as increased flash-flooding magnitude and frequency has caused havoc. On 23 November 2016 Barbados experienced over 170 mm of rainfall within a short period, which was only observed since 1955 due to the failure of the sewerage system.

The decreasing amount of freshwater available for consumption and increasing population demand will set the stage for increased drought which in turn further decrease groundwater recharge rates and increase salinization of aquifers, which provide 95% of the island's potable water supply. The current sustainable yield of aquifer suggests that sustainable aquifer yields could decrease by around 50% by 2050. This is because for Barbados as the level of groundwater abstraction is already between 85–100% of the safe yield.

## Project Summary and Climate Results

Water conservation, particularly through reclaimed water will be a critical component to help mitigate the impacts of climate change on water scarcity. Reclaimed water in wastewater that has been treated to tertiary treatment standards can be used for non-potable sources, such as agriculture, landscaping, turf maintenance, and recharging the aquifer.

The goal of 3R-CRWAS is to facilitate the enhancement of the health, wellbeing, and productivity of Barbadians by providing a sustainable water supply through the development of innovative technologies and strategies that ensure water is protected, managed, recycled, reused, and conserved. This will be achieved through four outcome level results and ten output level results.

Outcome 1 and 2 of this project are the largest in terms of budget, as they are focused on infrastructure works and technology development. Specifically, Outcome 1 will establish a resilient pathway through the upgrade of the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BTP) to a tertiary treatment system and the installation of a pipeline that will supply reclaimed water to support irrigation developments in the Codrington-North Leeward Valley, which will benefit 150 farmers on 235 hectares and recharge


---
**Page 10**
---

wells (replenish walls) for replenishing the aquifer. This water reclamation and reuse process will be complemented with the installation of decision support tools and infrastructure that are critical for resilient building.

Outcome 2, which spans the installation of renewable energy PV systems, automated controls such as variable frequency drives (VFDs) and a sludge dewatering system, will contribute to the achievement of a low-emission pathway.


Outcomes 3 and 4 are "soft" developmental results that primarily aim to create an enabling environment for achieving a paradigm shift in the approaches to the management of water resources, specifically wastewater, and having nation-wide public education and awareness on the nexus between climate change and water security. This will be achieved through developing and implementing Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) and Operational Manual and implementing a risk management framework.

Outcome 4 further strengthens the enabling environment through a legislative review, strengthening buy-in in providing adequate funding for wastewater treatment and reuse options and promoting transparency and lessons emerging from this project.


Outcomes 1 and 4 are expected to yield co-benefits related to social, economic, adaptation, mitigation and environmental sectors.

# B.1 Context and National Context

Barbados has a population of approximately 287,000 with a density in the region of 680 persons per km², making it one of the most densely populated countries in the world. Barbados is experiencing "absolute water scarcity" based on the Falkenmark water stress indicator since approximately 1950. The country has been ranked as the third most vulnerable to climate change among all small island developing states (SIDS) and the fourth most vulnerable among all countries in the world. Water availability in Barbados is acutely affected by the periods and intensities of the wet season, which can be very erratic and unpredictable so much so that a dry spell of only 10 days has the potential to reduce flows, thus influencing the livelihoods of farmers and water population and increasing their environmental vulnerability.

## Climate change projections for Barbados and impacts

As part of the IPCC Special Report on climate change projections using a multi-model CMIP5 ensemble with the SSP2-4.5 scenario and the epoch 2041–2060 indicate that the current issues and challenges faced by the country will be exacerbated in the future.

* The mean annual maximum temperature will increase by about +1.0°C.
* The mean annual minimum temperature will increase by about +1.3°C.
* The daily maximum temperature is largely projected to increase throughout the year.
* The number of heatwaves (three consecutive days where the maximum temperature exceeds the average daily maximum temperature at the 95th percentile) has increased over the period 1991–2000 and this trend will continue in future periods.

* Annual mean precipitation will decrease by about 9%. The decrease is particularly severe in the May – July period, which is the wettest time of year for Barbados.
* The number of days with heavy rainfall (daily total >50 mm) has increased over the period 1991–2000 and this trend will continue in future periods.

The results of the analysis on the climate change projections on temperature are aligned with the findings of Working Group I of the IPCC in their report for the Sixth Assessment Report (AR6). For precipitation and drought, the results are consistent with the findings from the AR5. The multi-model CMIP5 ensemble mean daily precipitation data for Barbados does show trends such as an increase in extremely heavy precipitation). From the analysis it is clear that there will be a decrease in total annual rainfall.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## INTENSITY OF EXTREME STORMS AND SEA-LEVEL RISE (SLR). Higher temperatures will affect the seasonality of crops, increase evaporation rates, induce heat stress on the population, including tourists and livestock, increase coastal erosion, and reduce water availability for agriculture and domestic use. This will also increase incidence of vector-borne diseases. Already, in recent years Barbados has experienced high incidences of vector borne and waterborne diseases that were attributed to episodic changes in temperature (Lo et al., 2013). The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to increase with sea level rise and increased storm surge flooding. It is projected there could be even more of an increase in water-borne diseases, due to limited time for recovery.

## MORE INTENSE RAINFALL, SEVERE STORM CONDITIONS AND SLR WILL PROMOTE FLASH FLOODING; SALINITY INTRUSION INTO AVAILABLE GROUNDWATER ABSTRACTION AND IRRIGATION WELLS AND SALINIZATION OF AGRICULTURAL LANDS; COASTAL EROSION AND LOSS OF CORAL REEFS. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are expected to increase with sea level rise and increased storm surge flooding, which has caused havoc in Barbados: In 29 November 2016 Barbados experienced over 170 mm of rainfall within a short period of time, causing severe flooding and damage to infrastructure such as roads and sewerage systems. Stormwater run-off from intense rainfall activity that contain debris, microorganisms and chemicals also contribute to nutrient loading in near-shore environments which negatively impact coral reefs. Barbados has the highest incidence of vector borne diseases in the Caribbean region. Coral reefs act as natural breakwaters along the coasts, also support more than 25% of all marine species, making them the most biologically diverse ecosystems on earth. The coral reefs are a major source of food and income for people on land. Furthermore, the coral reefs generate significant revenues for local fishermen and the tourism sector. Coral reefs provide protection against coastal erosion and storm surges. They also provide drinking water (9% of the total population) and indirectly contributes to about 40% of Barbados earnings1, making climate change a major threat.

The decreasing amount and increasingly skewed pattern of precipitation and increasing evaporation due to higher temperatures will increase water stressures and competition over water between tourism and other productive sectors, including agriculture and production. The aquifer system is the last stage in the hydrological cycle which further exacerbates the problem through reduced recharge rates and salinization of aquifers, which provide 95% of the island's potable water supply. At the onset of the 2016 wet season, Barbados experienced a severe drought that lasted for over six months. The drought was caused by low recharge and resulted in seawater intrusion into coastal aquifers and reduced yields (see Figure I). The full extent of this problem is not yet known but it has been estimated that if nothing is done to increase recharge by 2050 under an RCP 2.6 scenario, groundwater yields could be reduced to 26.9 Mm/year, and 25.5 Mm/year for the RCP 8.5 scenario (see Figure I). The current yield of potable water is approximately 137 Mm/year and 82.3 Mm/year, depending on which estimate is used2. Studies which took a mass-balance approach to characterizing groundwater recharge in Barbados found that the average annual recharge rate was progressively depleted4. The overall implication of the available body of work suggests that sustainable aquifer yields could decrease by around 50% by 2050. This is modelling for Barbados as the island's groundwater replenishment is already between 10-30% of its safe yield.

Recall that a lower estimate of safe yield given above was 18 Mm³/year, which would have been sufficient to meet demand in 2030, with losses approximately 20.0 Mm³/year or some 38% of production. Even removing all losses from the distribution system, which is unrealistic, would not be sufficient to offset the projected impact of climate change on aquifer yield and quality.

Interventions to counter the impacts of climate change

As a result of the projected effects of climate change on water quality and availability in Barbados, coupled with the increasing trend towards tourism development that attracts more tourist arrivals, it will be necessary for Barbados to not only develop current wastewater management practices but also to consider new approaches. This includes developing strategies to reduce demand for potable water and identify new sources of water that can be used to recharge the underground aquifers to be later used as potable water or for other productive activities where water quality is not required.

It is estimated that approximately 80-90% of the water used for domestic activity is discharged as wastewater. This represents a sustainable source of wastewater that could be treated and used as reclaimed water in other productive activities, where potable water quality is not required. In October 2018, the

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

Government of Barbados (GOB) enacted a National Water Reuse Policy (NWRP), which states that "The traditional interpretation of the term water must expand beyond conventional freshwater sources to encompass treated wastewater (reclaimed water), storm water and other sources as part of the total water resources."

There are two municipal wastewater treatment facilities: the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) and the South Coast Sewage Treatment Plant (SCSTP). The 30-year-old BSTP has approximately 1,500 connections. The SCSTP is a relatively new facility with a capacity of 200,000 gallons per day. Primary treatment works on an ocean outfall and accommodates approximately 3,000 connections, of which there are only about 600 in operation due to the failure of a lift distribution system installed, but it never operated properly and was abandoned many years ago.

According to the 2010 population census, whilst virtually 100% of all households in Barbados are connected to water supply systems, only approximately 50% have access to sanitation. The remaining households use pit latrines, septic tanks and septic tank systems. In some instances, treated wastewater is reused for landscaping purposes - currently there are 68 such sites in operation. Apart from the latter scenarios, there is little reuse of treated wastewater in Barbados.

The infrastructure and facilities in place to manage water resources are also vulnerable to climate change. For example, during a major storm event, the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) will be shut down for 24 hours or more due to flooding. Without power supply to pumping stations, water supply to households is also affected. In addition, if there is a hurricane or other climatic factor that disrupts power supply to pumping stations, water supply to households can be affected.

In the event of a major storm event, the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) will be shut down for 24 hours or more due to flooding. Without power supply to pumping stations, water supply to households is also affected. In addition, if there is a hurricane or other climatic factor that disrupts power supply to pumping stations, water supply to households can be affected.

Events of climate parameters that can affect wastewater infrastructure and events include:

### Precipitation
- High intensity, frequency and duration of precipitation events leading to infrastructure flooding and overflow conditions;
- Increased likelihood and frequency of sewer flooding, overflows, and spills;
- Increased risk of erosion and introduction of sediment to sewers;
- Excessive loading to wastewater sewage treatment works; and
- Surface water runoff into the system which can reduce the efficiency and efficacy of onsite wastewater treatment systems such as soak-away fields.

### Wind
- Increased wind leading on infrastructure assets and buildings.

### Temperature
- High waves leading to reduced water availability and higher sewage contaminant concentrations (less dilution) increased sewer related odor generation and release;
- Increased risk of corrosion due to product resulting in increased infrastructure damage due to corrosion; and
- Increased environmental impacts of residual contaminants including nutrient impacts due to elevated receiving water temperatures.


---
**Page 15**
---

Ocean and Geotechnical

* Increased incidents of storm surges affecting wastewater discharge and property flooding;
* Increased salination impacting geotechnical stability to support tanks and other infrastructure as well as affected the effectiveness of wastewater treatment processes and soil erosion in affected areas.

The impacts of climate change on groundwater resources also extends to on-site wastewater disposal, for such practices in specific fields. On-site ground disposal of wastewater relies on the soil being unsaturated to effect natural biological, physical, and chemical treatment processes and protect groundwater quality. Such systems are vulnerable to flooding events that can lead to over-saturation of the soil, allowing pollutants to travel long distances through the soil, as well as result in surface failure and break-out of wastewater on the surface with potential health risks.

To address these impacts, three Fit Five (Reduce, Reuse and Recycle) for Climate Resilience Wastewater Systems in Barbados (R3-CWb5) project proposes to not only support the adaptation of Barbados to the water scarcity conditions that will be amplified by climate variability and change, but to also reduce the environmental footprint of wastewater systems. The R3-CWb5 project is designed to create a resilient water and wastewater management system; treated wastewater reuse and aquifer recharge strategies are being developed as part of this project.

The SR-CWb5 will benefit from the lessons of the following projects and the project management team, which have been involved in the development of the R3-CWb5. The following resources and tools were leveraged:

* The GOB received a loan from the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB) for the Water and Sanitation Systems Upgrade of the BWA that ran from 2011 to 2016. The project supported the modernization of the water supply system, including the rehabilitation of the existing water production infrastructure and the efficiency of the operations of the BWA. This project is benefiting from the work done towards wastewater management such as the preparation of the wastewater reuse master plan.
* The Global Environment Facility (GEF) funded a Testing a Prototype Caribbean Regional Fund for Water Management (CRFWM) project in Barbados, Guyana and Jamaica between 2013-2016. The project used an integrated and innovative approach to reduce the negative environmental and human health impacts of water management practices on the environment. Barbados is not a beneficiary country of this pilot project; however, the noted challenges and key lessons coming out of this pilot project are being leveraged in the R3-CWb5 project.
* There is an ongoing follow-up project called the CRFW+ and Barbados is a beneficiary country, set to be fully operational by 2019. The CRFW+ project will support the development of the International Water and Wastewater Management (IWWM), Sustainable and tailor-made financing options for urban, peri-urban and rural IWWM, in Provision of innovative small-scale, total, rural, peri-urban and combined water supply systems, and in Capacity building and awareness raising on IWWM. The project will focus on the importance of IWWM order to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals. All 17 components of this project are being leveraged by the R3-CWb5 project which aims at improving the living environment and awareness of wastewater as a resource at the community level and promotes private sector investment in water and sanitation infrastructure, including the development of the SR-CWb5 project.
* The Water Sector Resilience Nexus for Sustainability in Barbados (WSR-S-Barbados) project, which is funded by the GCF (P906), includes the following complementary results: (i) making the island’s water sector more resilient to climate change and disasters; (ii) improving the island's drinking water supply; and (iii) establishing a revolving fund strictly for supporting climate

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WASTEWATER RECLAMATION PROJECT (SCWRP)

## Background

The South Coast Water Reclamation Project (SCWRP) was initiated in 2015 and aimed at transforming the existing South Coast Plant, into a future Water Resource Recovery Facility (WRRF). The SCWRP is designed to address several critical issues including: (i) mitigating climate change effects; (ii) addressing seawater intrusion and sea level rise; (iii) diversifying water resources; and (iv) ensuring the sustainability of treated effluent on marine life and the environment. At the time of this proposal writing, this project was in the inception phase but is expected to be fully implemented at the start of the SC-WRRF.

## Risks and Barriers

While some degree of work has been advanced for the water sector, including wastewater; this project is different from other projects in that there are barriers that have hindered the government of Barbados to transform it into a wastewater sector. These include:

- **Financial**: Barbados has a high debt-to-GDP ratio - at the end of 2020 it was 144%. With the prolonged effects of COVID-19 on the tourism sector and increased government spending on health care in response, the country is facing significant financial challenges. Despite these challenges, the debt response to the COVID-19 pandemic and adverse development and climate change projections, Barbados has been able to secure financing for this project from the Green Climate Fund. The Government of Barbados, the use of reclaimed water is becoming a critical resource.


---
**Page 17**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

B

**reclaimed water for productive/economic purposes; however, the adoption of upgraded treatment and process technologies is necessary. The SR-CRWs project is a part of this paradigm shift. The SR-CRWs project will be implemented by GCF funding, however, the overall planned investment for transforming the sector, the GCF contribution represents only a fifth of the planned investment. Further, the ownership and sustainability dimensions are critical and is the assumption that the private sector will be involved in the operation and maintenance of the wastewater treatment systems, which is, and will continue to be, a significant part of the operational costs.**

## Social: The underlying paradigm shift of this project is a change in culture and attitude towards the use of water at the household level; however, there is limited knowledge and awareness about the usefulness of reclaimed water for productive purposes. This project aims to promote an environmentally sound SR-CRWs will enhance the knowledge and awareness of the public, including visitors (tourists) and the private sector to change current behaviour to better manage and conserve water or accept the use of reclaimed water. The project will also be part of a national awareness programme and a strategy targeting the engagement of the private sector.

## Institutional: There is lack of trained staff in terms of the implementation of the upgrades planned by for the BWA, as well as the capacity to run the plant, manage and maintain wastewater systems implemented. The SR-CRWs was specially designed to promote a paradigm shift in the institutional arrangements through the provision of specialized training to BWA and key stakeholders, including from the private sector.

## Technological: There is inadequate up-to-date technologies in place at the BSTP to treat waste water at household level and to support the BWA's operations in a manner that is effective, efficient and that promotes energy conservation. One of the reasons for this lack of technology is only 1 flow meter that was recently installed in December 2021, and prior to this there was a large gap in terms of monitoring and management of the plant. The SR-CRWs project will ensure state-of-the-art equipment and technologies are implemented at the BSTP, including solar power generation for solar power to meet the energy demands of the upgrades, whilst also withstanding at least category 3 hurricane force winds and other extreme weather conditions such as flooding, drought, intense and frequent hurricane season for the Caribbean. Further, SR-CRWs will be implementing decision support systems that will provide real-time monitoring and control of plant operations, maintenance and management, and better response to the changes in the hydraulic capacity during storm events.

## Regulatory: While there is very recently updated legislation (last quarter of 2021) that speaks to the use of wastewater treatment systems and reclaimed water; some legislations have not gotten approval yet. Further, the lack of enforcement is a serious issue since there is already evidence of non-compliance with existing regulations. In order to ensure that this project will undergo a gap assessment to determine if any gaps exist across the emerging policies and legislation to ensure that the paradigm shift regarding the use of wastewater technologies and reclaimed water can be achieved. The SR-CRWs project will also be implementing an accompanying roadmap that articulates how these emerging policies and legislation should be promoted and implemented.

# Background and Context

Small Island Developing States (SIDS), such as Barbados, are directly exposed to the extreme impacts of climate change. By their very nature, sea-level rise (SLR) has a significant impact on coastal zones, where most of the population tends to be concentrated. SLR is to be considered in all aspects of long-term national planning, and more so in planning for critical public infrastructure which tends to have a high level of exposure to SLR. The Government of Barbados has already taken steps to protect assets or accommodate SLR diminishing, forcing solutions based on a retreat from the coastal zone (see Figure 2 below). This is done by placing a premium priority on an increase in water and accommodation space through upgrading the implementation of the freshwater aquifer and thus save salinization, arguably the most pressing impact of climate change on Barbados currently as virtually all freshwater resources are groundwater. This will create more time for the Government to respond to the inevitable impacts of SLR.

## Figure 2: Decreasing solution space for small islands as sea level rises
Source: Hasnoot et. al. 2021. "Pathways to coastal retreat", Science, v.374(6548), pp.1287-1290

Wastewater management strategies using reclaimed water will be a critical component to help mitigate impacts on groundwater levels, tightly linked to overall island water resources. Tertiary treatment of wastewater is required for the removal of pathogens and other contaminants that can cause disease transmission, maintenance, and recharging the aquifer. Secondary treated sludge can be used as fertilizer for the agricultural community and activated sludge can be used in landscaping, turf maintenance of lawns, recreational areas, etc.

The goal of 3R-CRWWS project is to facilitate the enhancement of the health, wellbeing, and productivity of Barbadians through the use of carbon neutral and climate resilient water and energy management technologies and strategies that ensures water is protected, managed, recycled, reused, and conserved.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 13

## B

### Key Components of the 3R-CReWS Project

1. Increase knowledge of wastewater generation including sources of wastewater and the quality and quantity impacts, as well optimize treatment and minimize energy consumption and associated GHG emissions.
2. Build capacity into Barbados' wastewater management systems, which results in increased water availability, production, distribution, and access thereby improving the community's resilience, health, and economic development.
3. Produce a treated wastewater effluent quality so that it can be reused for agricultural purposes, reducing stress on diminishing groundwater resources and possible water supplies as a result of climate change.
4. Enable the use of reclaimed water for aquifer recharge thereby reducing saline intrusion and contamination in coastal areas, which will enhance freshwater availability during periods of droughts for Barbados.
5. Reduce energy costs by increasing self-sufficiency, contribute to the electricity grid and act as a power shortfall filler through the installation of solar panels on existing buildings and new construction.
6. Increase supply of locally sourced renewable energy that allows for a long-term source of revenue through the sale of excess electricity generated from solar panels.
7. Provide a standard and formal guidance to regulate and promote the use of reclaimed water and efficient wastewater treatment systems in Barbados.
8. Build capacity and re-train BWA staff, including the private sector, to conduct preventive maintenance and adopt climate-risk related adaptation strategies to increase the wastewater collection and treatment system's resilience against extreme weather events.
9. Promote and demonstrate actions that encourage all water users in Barbados, including tourists, to conserve water through the installation of low-flow fixtures and other water-saving devices.
10. Strengthen the enabling environment and strengthening buy-in, ownership and leadership in the private sector by engaging them as partners in the project implementation process.

For achieving the goals and specific objectives, the 3R-CReWS is organised into four (4) key components as summarized in Figure 3.

## Summary of the Components of 3R-CReWS

These four components are synonymous with the following four outcomes of the project:

1. Enhanced availability, management and use of tertiary level reclaimed water to improve the water sector's resilience to climate change.
2. Climate resilient wastewater services achieved at BSTP.
3. Enhanced capacity and capability to support a preventative maintenance (PM) and climate resiliency program.
4. An enabling environment is created for wastewater technologies and use of reclaimed water.

Collectively, these four outcomes/components are designed in consideration of the barriers that exist in achieving a paradigm shift in the adoption of wastewater treatment technologies and reuse options in South Africa. These barriers include limited access to tertiary level reclaimed water, limited technical capacities in the public and private sectors to fully maintain and advance the adoption of wastewater technologies, limited knowledge and awareness about the usefulness of reclaimed water as a resource for sustainable development, and limited institutional capacity and policy frameworks that promote the adoption of wastewater treatment technologies and reuse options, particularly at small-scale.

Outcomes 1 of this project is the largest, in terms of budget, since it is primarily focused on the upgrades to the BSTP and transfer of technology. Specifically, Outcome 1 is made up of four (4) outputs. Output 1 aims to achieve a climate resilient pathway for the water sector through the upgrade of the BSTP to a tertiary treatment plant with an additional RO membrane. This output supports the achievement of GCF's ARA2 and will result in co-benefits, which include improving the quality in the near shore and marine environments, increase water available for the

### B.2 Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

As set out in Section B.2 the 3R-CRWs project has four (4) components:

- **Component 1** involves upgrading centralizing and installing cluster wastewater collection and treatment systems. The main output of this component are: (1) The Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) upgraded to treat wastewater at a tertiary water quality standard; (2) Recycled water being used for irrigation, cooling, toilet flushing, and public space cleaning and supply recharge; (3) Decisions making tools implemented to mitigate climate change risks to the wastewater treatment and collection systems; (4) Onsite decentralized package treatment plants or cluster wastewater treatment systems.

- **Component 2** intends to enhance the climate resilience and carbon-neutrality of the BSTP exploiting existing infrastructure and upgrading the plant with energy efficient equipment. The main output of this component is improved emergency power and energy efficiency reduction in emissions at the BSTP.

- **Component 3** involves creating a enabling environment for wastewater management through the development of standard operating procedures manual, flow and water quality monitoring, and institutional strengthening to enhance the technical and managerial capabilities of water technical personnel to operate, maintain and monitor and implement climate change adaptation planning strategies for wastewater management.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 19

## Component 4: Issues on educating the public and private sector on the importance of wastewater reclamation and address climate change issues affecting the water sector and enabling policy and legislation.

The outputs of this component are:
- (1) Governance and planning frameworks updated to include wastewater reclamation and reuse applications/ systems developed; and,
- (3) Gender Sensitive Public Education and Awareness Campaigns

### Component 1: Wastewater Reclamation and Reuse

Barbados is almost entirely dependent on groundwater supplies (85% of potable water supply comes from groundwater). The current rate of abstraction is estimated at approximately 40% of the total body of work suggests that sustainable aquifer yields could decrease by around 50% by 2050. This is troubling as the indicators are that the level of groundwater abstraction is already at between 85-100% of the sustainable yield. The current rate of recharge is estimated to be approximately 30% of total water distribution and an estimated 11 Mm³ is extracted for agricultural irrigation[1]. The exact amount of water entering aquifers is not known.

The outcomes of this component are enhanced availability, management and use of tertiary level reclaimed water to improve the water sector’s resilience to climate change. The BSTP will be upgraded in two communities in Zone 2. The BSTP will be upgraded to treat wastewater to a tertiary reuse quality standard. The effluent from the upgraded plants will be utilized for non-potable water applications including groundwater augmentation and irrigation. This will result in reduced salinization, reduced aquifer saltwater intrusion, and increased availability of groundwater during dry periods. This intervention is expected to have positive impacts on women who predominantly undertake domestic and care roles. There will also be monitoring systems implemented to address data gaps that exist and to facilitate enhanced management of the upgraded BSTP.

The total budget of this outcome is US$29 million, which is principally applied to the upgrades associated with wastewater treatment. The source of the funding is GCF in the amount of US$29.2 million (grant) and BWA in the amount of US$930K.

### Output 1.1: The Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant is upgraded to treat wastewater to a tertiary water-quality standard.

The BSTP is currently a secondary level treatment plant with water being released into the marine environment through the outfall. The BSTP will be upgraded to treat wastewater to a tertiary reuse water quality standard using the existing infrastructure and wastewater treatment process (Conventional Activated Sludge). The upgrade will include the installation of tertiary treatment equipment and the development of non-potable water applications including groundwater augmentation and irrigation. Figure 5 below depicts the existing BSTP and Figure 6 is a schema of the existing and upgraded system that has been agreed to by the GDB (see Annex 1). The project will also include monitoring systems which are expected to address the water security considering the projected climate change impacts on water resources for the island.

Activity 1.1.1: Design, procure and convert/upgrade the existing conventional activated sludge (CAS) biological treatment process at the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant to tertiary filtration and disinfection for achieving national reclaimed water-quality standards.

Output 1.2: Tertiary treated reclaimed water is available to supplement non-potable use
Sea level rise and salt-water intrusion of potable water aquifers coupled with changing weather patterns (intermittent and higher intensity rainfall) stresses Barbados water resources. In addition, agriculture continues to be the largest consumer of freshwater in Barbados. The demand for fresh water has increased and the GoB has increased its call for greater domestic food production through new and improved methods of farming. This will increase the demand on freshwater availability. Using reclaimed water, for irrigation purposes, should also lead to improved food security.

The supply of treated reclaimed wastewater to support irrigation developments in the Codrington-Nels-Lears-Saltons-Constant-Valley was deemed most suitable for this project (See Figure 7) and will provide

B


benefits to all farmers (60% males and 40% males). In motivating and recommending this Option, the GCF stressed that this should be seen as a first Phase of a larger enterprise to extend and expand the use of treated reclaimed wastewater for irrigation purposes on lands along the west coast of Barbados and up to the north coast. The reclaimed water produced at the Bstp is currently used in agriculture. However, this reuse requires reverse osmosis membrane filtration to remove total dissolved solids to a concentration less than 400 mg/l. This process is carried out by the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Production.

Activity 1.2: Install reverse osmosis (RO) membrane filtration systems to reduce the total dissolved solids concentration of the reclaimed water produced at the BSTP

Activity 1.2.2: Install a 9km pipeline and 6 aquifer recharge wells (injection wells) going from the BSTP for irrigation and aquifer recharge.

Output 1.3: Decision-support tools and infrastructure implemented to mitigate potential climate change risks to the wastewater collection and treatment systems

Decision-support tools (DSTs) and infrastructure upgrades are critical to enhance adaptive management to climate change. Storm surges and rising sea levels can cause saltwater entry into the wastewater collection infrastructure, which in turn can lead to contamination of reclaimed water. This will reduce the quality of reuse water, which warrants the need for a sewer monitoring programme. This will allow for greater understanding of flows in the wet and dry season as well as the identification of points of stormwater

## Infiltration into the collection system, considering the hydraulic capacity of the BSTP to inform remedial actions.

Several decision-support tools and infrastructure are also needed to optimize the preventative maintenance programme for the BSTP. The BSTP will be equipped with a data acquisition system that will replace the one by the fire in the past; an electronic Computerized Real-time Management System (CMMIS) information system will enable important information to be stored and readily accessible for analysis and will be less susceptible to potential damage from flooding or other natural disasters. The BSTP will also be equipped with a water quality monitoring system to monitor GHG emissions associated with the operations of the plant.

There is also need for periodic monitoring of the quality of the reclaimed water to manage associated environmental impacts. This monitoring will be done on effluent water quality through the establishment of a laboratory, to optimize operations control strategies and reduce energy consumption and GHG emissions as well provide data as part of the public visibility programme to build their confidence and awareness. The BSTP will also be equipped with a water quality monitoring system to monitor the BOD concentration of reclaimed wastewater if less than 450 mg/L.

Output 1.3 - Decentralized treatment systems or cluster treatment systems installed

Under this output climate resilience, water security, safeguarding of groundwater quality and achieving a paradigm shift in the use of reclaimed water will be promoted through the installation of decentralized treatment systems in communities. The implementation of decentralized treatment systems in communities will provide enhanced collection and treatment systems that will produce reclaimed water suitable for agricultural irrigation, livestock watering, and other non-potable uses (based on the different wastewater systems being used). This output will also expand the number of beneficiaries of this project in a case where there is no centralized treatment plant. The cost of installing decentralized treatment plants in operation in Barbados and the cost of distributing reclaimed water from the BSTP into the community is high.

The implementation of the decentralized treatment systems will safeguard groundwater by eliminating the potential risk from contamination associated with onsite wastewater disposal (pit latrines, septic tanks, and soak pits). This will reduce the amount of nutrients entering the aquifer, thereby improving the soil quality, reducing the ability of the bacteria in the soil to treat the wastewater, and potentially causing the water supply to be contaminated. The decentralized treatment systems will also provide a service to the public. This output is part of the GCB's larger programme to expand decentralized treatment systems into communities, with the purpose of providing access to reclaimed water for households not connected to centralized treatment plants or to allow households to be connected to the network of reclaimed water pipelines and address public health concerns.

### Activity 1.4.1: Construct two small (cluster) decentralized wastewater collection and treatment demonstration projects in Zone A locations to produce reuse quality water for domestic/commercial non-potable water applications.

## Component 2: Renewable Energy and Energy Efficiency in Wastewater Treatment

Currently, the Bridgeport Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) relies on electricity supply from conventional power plants that use fossil resources, which not only increases operational costs but also contributes to global warming. The BSTP currently uses 4,500 kWh of energy per day which results in approximately 12 tons of CO2 emissions annually. This is equivalent to the reduction of direct GHG emissions from the treatment process (at an average flow of 4 Ml/day) to approximately 375 kg/day.

The outcome of this component is that climate resilient low carbon operations achieved at BSTP. This will be realized by: 1) installing grid-fed Photovoltaic (PV) Renewable Energy Systems to offset increased power demand; and 2) implementing energy efficiency measures within the upgraded centralized treatment processes to reduce the overall energy footprint and reduce GHG emissions associated with the biosolids.

The total budget of this outcome is USD $3.3 million, which is principally applied to the design and installation of a 1 MWp PV system and the existing grid-fed PV system (40 kWp). The project will be funded by GCF.

### Outcome 2.1: Energy efficiency and renewable energy technologies are implemented

Currently, the BSTP has a peak power demand of approximately 13.8 kW which is required to accommodate the upgrades to the BSTP and operate the RO plant to achieve the water quality standard set for agriculture reuse purposes. This output will deliver 1 MW grid-fed PV system to offset the energy demand of the existing grid-fed PV system (40 kWp) and the new 1 MWp PV system, resulting in an estimated GHG emission reduction of 1,411 kgCO2e per year (or 35.725 tCO2e over 25 years). For more details, see Feasibility Study for Grid-Fed Photovoltaic System at BSTP. The existing grid-fed PV system will be upgraded to automate the supply from the grid to help manage intermittency and switching over between the grid and solar PV system. It is currently envisioned that PV panels will be installed at the BSTP (See Feasibility Study for Grid-Fed Photovoltaic System at BSTP). In addition, a 1 MWp PV system will also be considered as an alternative off-site installation of the PV panel. One such is the Bells Pumping Station. If the latter is selected, it would require additional grid connection and interconnection with the existing grid-fed PV system.

The proposed 1 MWp PV system at BSTP will consist of 320 mono-crystalline modules with a capacity of 5 kW each. The system will be installed on the roof of the existing building with previous PV system installation by the BWA. High efficient and durable modules such as mono-crystalline modules will be installed. There is an existing invertor room at BSTP but this will need to be replaced with a new one that can handle 1 MWp power output. The existing grid-fed PV system and the inverter room will routed to the switch gear at the BSTP. This power will be coupled with the main bus bar system and connected to the RO plant. The existing grid-fed PV system will be decommissioned from the BSTP or with the Power Utility System. Approvals are required from the Government Electrical Engineering Department (GEED), Barbados Light and Power (BL&P) and the Division of Energy.

Besides the 1 MWp grid-fed PV system, this project involves the implementation of energy efficient technologies and sludge dewatering equipment. This will further reduce the overall energy footprint and

THIS WILL RESULT IN A REDUCTION OF GHG EMISSIONS ASSOCIATED WITH SOLID MANAGEMENT THROUGH REDUCTION LABOUR AND THE ENVIRONMENTAL BENEFITS INCLUDING THE REDUCTION IN GHG EMISSIONS AND ALL SPILLS ET.

### Component 3: Capabilities to operate, maintain, expand and monitor wastewater and related renewable energy technologies

This component responds to the need for greater understanding among BWA staff of the nexus between climate change adaptation and water management. It also aims to build capacity in terms of technical measures to mitigate these impacts. Additionally, over the past few years the maintenance focus at BWA has been on the treatment plants located in the north coast of Barbados due to heavy rainfall events, and the lack of dedicated staff and financial resources for PM. Further, the west coast of Barbados is not severely affected by climate change and the impact of saltwater intrusion into freshwater resources and nutrient loading from agricultural runoff are minimal. This component will address this knowledge gap and chart a path for addressing those issues on the west coast.

The outcome of this component is to have enhanced capacity and capability to support the preventative maintenance and climate resiliency programmes.

### Output 3.1: Improved capabilities of wastewater technical personnel to operate, maintain and monitor and implement climate change adaptation planning strategies for wastewater management

To complement the upgrades planned for the BSTP (Component 1 and 2), specialized training, enhanced monitoring equipment and improved laboratory facilities will be provided in order for BWA staff to be able to effectively manage, monitor and maintain the upgraded system. Not only are these areas important for ensuring that the BSTP is implemented as designed but they also provide a platform for implementing the preventative maintenance programme and ensuring sustainability of its operations post implementation of this project. See Figure 11 for the key areas that will be targeted for enhancing BWA's capabilities in wastewater management. These include training, capacity building, climate change adaptation planning strategies, CV systems, laboratory testing, analysis and management, CAS and RO technologies, sewer monitoring and maintenance, and water quality monitoring.

It is envisaged that approximately 400 individuals will be trained. Where possible, the Project will aim for parity in the number of individuals trained; nevertheless, women will be strongly encouraged to participate in the technical training programs thereby addressing the decision-making gap for women in technical thematic areas.

Component 4: Capacities (regulatory, governance, awareness), buy-in and ownership within the private and public sectors are improved for climate resilient development planning for the water sector

This upgrades to the equipment and training of technical staff to manage them is not enough for a lasting and sustainable impact of this project. Stakeholders buy-in; adequate enforcement of legislative and governance frameworks; and ensuring that there is a culture of partnership and professionalism within the water sector in Barbados, are required to ensure that a sustainable market transformation occurs for the advancement of wastewater treatment and use of reclaimed water in Barbados. For example, the legal regulatory framework in Barbados was very restrictive on the use of reclaimed water as it was considered unsafe for human consumption or contact with drinking water. However, support is required to determine if any gaps, including gender and vulnerability gaps exist against this framework and to ensure that there is a clear specification of a roadmap for addressing the gaps and enforcing the policies and regulations that are in place.

Further, to ensure the best development outcome of the project, the project and potential impacts should be well understood by all stakeholders involved throughout the project cycle, especially those disproportionately impacted by climate change such as youth and women.

### Activity 4.23: Developing and Revolving Adaptation Fund Facility (RAFF) to provide resources for the adoption of climate change adaptation actions

Barriers to financial inclusion or access to financial resources can prevent vulnerable groups, including female headed households from participating in climate change adaptation and/or mitigation actions. The Barbados Water Resources Sectoral Action Plan (BWSRAP) is establishing a Water Sectoral Adaptation Fund Facility (WRSRF-S-Barbados). Project is establishing the Revolving Adaptation Fund Facility (RAFF). The RAFF aims to mobilise funds for use in the water sector adaptation and mitigation initiatives including those identified as part of the BWSRAP. The RAFF will be structured around the following components:

1. Empower communities to adapt to climate change and variability.
2. Mobilise funds for climate change adaptation and mitigation in Barbados.
3. Strengthen households and public and private sector entities involvement in climate actions.
4. Address water sector needs for potable water by building resilience to climate change into the infrastructure of the BWA.
5. Promote technology transfer by encouraging cost-effective, efficient, and appropriate technologies for the water sector and other sectors.
6. Build on existing climate change capacity building and awareness campaigns.

The establishment of the RAFF was initially funded by the BWRA and Government of Barbados; however, 3R-CRWTS presents an opportunity to expand and promote the RAFF to support the Private Sector's engagement in climate change adaptation and mitigation actions through the private sector's increased utilization of tertiary reclaimed water. The RAFF is seen as a critical element to engage the private sector; however, it will be necessary for the RAFF to be structured around the following components: in and out to support activities related to the adoption of decentralized treatment systems as well as dual purpose systems that can provide both potable and non-potable water. The RAFF is also supported by the BWRA. This Charter is to be updated to cover wastewater treatment systems and reuse options and other water sector considerations. Specifically, the 3R-CRWTS will engage consultants to update the governance instrument for the RAFF. The RAFF will be structured around the following components: increase the capital inflow; ensure that updates to the governing instrument continue to promote the needs of vulnerable groups including persons living in poverty, and support the knowledge and promotion of the tripling facility.

It is further noted that no GCF proceeds will be used to directly capitalise the incentive programme or the RAFF. GCF resources will be used to strengthen the framework and create the knowledge products

The CCCC as an executing entity will manage, procure and account for all activities associated with the GCF proceeds as well as monitor and report on the core project activities to the GCF and Government of Barbados. The CCCC will also be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out in accordance with the terms set out in the Subsidiary Agreement and the FAA, including those related to the BWA. The BWA PMO to provide support in areas such as project implementation, monitoring and evaluation, reporting, communication and interagency coordination during project implementation, and to act as a catalyst for an ongoing coordination mechanism after implementation has been completed. The Committee will be required to meet at least once every six months to review progress against the approved work plan and to address any emerging key challenges.

The BWA PMO will be required to report to the General Manager of BWA, who in turn will report to the BWA Board of Directors and the Project Steering Committee (PSC). Additionally, the GCCCF PMO will also report to the PSC. The PSC will be responsible for providing strategic direction on project implementation and interagency coordination during project implementation, and to act as a catalyst for an ongoing coordination mechanism after implementation has been completed. The Committee will be required to meet at least once every six months to review progress against the approved work plan and to address any emerging key challenges.

The PSC will be chaired by the Executive Director of the Cuban Caribbean Climate Change Centre and co-chaired by one of the Permanent Secretaries (PM) or Project Coordinator (PC) serving as Secretary, who will be responsible for taking minutes for each PSC meeting and shared with the relevant stakeholders. The PSC will consist of:

(a) Permanent Secretary (PS), Ministry of Transport Works and Water Resources Management, or their nominee;

(b) PS, Ministry of Agriculture and Food and Nutritional Security or their nominee;
(c) PS, Ministry of Finance Economic Affairs and Investment or their nominee;

(g) Director, Planning and Development Department, or their nominee;

## Project Management and Reporting Structure for the project

Financial Flows and Implementation Arrangements

The funding for the 3R-CRW/S project will come from: (1) the Green Climate Fund in the form of a grant; and (2) the BWA in the form of a grant from the Government of Barbados. The BWA contribution will be provided as a loan to the CCCCCC, which will then provide it to the BWA Project Management Office. The CCCCCC has already sourced some of the co-financing required including plans to allocate resources to the BWA from the revenue generated by the Garbage and Sewage Contribution levy to fund the two decentralised wastewater treatment plant (Output 1.4).

CCCCCC will not on grant any monies to the BWA nor will BWA on grant monies to the CCCCCC. CCCCCC will manage, procure and account for all activities associated with the GCF proceeds. The CCCCCC will procure all goods and services required in accordance with the procurement plan. The CCCCCC will also use a payment method in accordance with the procurement plan. The CCCCCC in accordance with its Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) with the GCF, will be responsible for managing all financial transactions and services for the realisation of the project outputs. As is CCCCCC policy, funding provided by the GCF is kept in a separate project bank account. The partners providing co-financing, will manage their co-financing accounts independently from the CCCCCC. The CCCCCC will not be responsible for any financial transactions and is not obligated to transfer any monies to the CCCCCC but must provide accounting evidence of transactions and services provided by the BWA or financing obligations. These are essential for monitoring and reporting to the GCF on an annual basis.

## Financial Flows, Including the System Upgrades that are Parallel Financing
The 35-Cent's project builds upon the Centre’s more than 15 years of experience as a regional leader implementing catalytic pilot/demonstration type projects in the Caribbean Region, and scaling these up with national governments. The Centre has also been involved in several other projects related to climate change adaptation and mitigation. Further, as an Accredited Entity of the GCF, the Centre does ensure that appropriate due diligence is carried out to ensure that all transactions are executed with integrity and transparency. The Centre has a robust internal control system for managing its financial risks.

The due diligence exercised revealed that the CCCCCC has sufficient capacity and autonomy to enter into Arrangements with other parties in order to implement projects, including the 35-Cent's project. The due diligence, the CCCCCC is properly constituted, and with Board approvals, is competent in taking on the tasks assigned by the GCF. The Centre has a strong track record of implementing projects in Barbados and working with the BWA through its PMO that is implementing another GCF funded project (P056): Water Sector Resilience Nexus for Sustainability in Barbados Project (WSRN-S-Barbados).

The BWA is responsible for managing, allocating and monitoring the water resources of the island with the very limited infrastructure available. The BWA supplies 97% of the island’s population with potable water; the facilities of the Authority are situated in a number of locations across the country and these include pumping stations, reservoirs and major storage centres at Bowmanston, St John and the Bele, St Michael. The BWA supplies 97% of the island with water and obtains its water supply from twenty four (24) groundwater wells.

B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Barbados Economic State of Play

In recent years, Barbadian economy remains sluggish and the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic has worsened this plight. Inflation rates have been around 4 per cent over the last 4 years and is projected to remain at around 3 per cent in the medium term. A combination of economic circumstances and a global supply chain disruption has led to an increase in import prices, which has contributed to the COVID-19 pandemic-led to a situation where public debt-to-GDP ratio increased consistently from 50% of GDP in 2017 to over 63% at end of 2020. At the end of the first quarter of 2021 Barbadian’s GDP is estimated to have contracted by 17.3% and the current account deficit is expected to have increased to 19.5%. The unemployment rate rose from 13.6% as of September 2019 following public sector layoffs of almost 1,000 persons in 2018 stemming from


---
**Page 46**
---

The introduction of the Barbados Economic Recovery and Transformation (BERT) Programme™[1]. With the onset of the pandemic, unemployment rates increased further [2] to [3].

Further, Barbados has been increasing in the proportion of the population living in poverty from 15.1% in 2010 to 17.2% in 2019[4]. The country has also experienced a rise in food insecurity and malnutrition no longer seen since 2016, which is likely that the poverty rate increased in the two years leading up to 2020, in line with the observed economic slowdown due to job losses, business closures, and decline in remittances[5].

The country also experienced a surge of COVID-19 cases from mid-March 2021 until early January 2021 and again in August 2021, with the latter rates including the Delta variant that is more contagious than previous variants. The country has been experiencing a decline in new daily cases since late September 2021[6], with a total of 354 new daily cases being reported for the month of October 2021 were in the 300s pushing Barbados to be the fourth country in the world for the 7-day incidence rate (data report on 8 October 2021)[7].

On March 20, 2020, the Prime Minister announced a complement of social, monetary and economic policy measures aimed at mitigating the impact of COVID-19. The total budgetary allocation for this was USD35 million which was allocated for social assistance to allow for an increase in the transfers paid by the Welfare Fund[8]. This was also coupled with a reduction in the amount of money that the government pays out for households that had cut it off. Monetary policy changes included the Central Bank of Barbados reducing its discount rate from 10% to 5%, which is the lowest level since 2009, and increased the liquidity ratio by 3 percentage points[9], and eliminated the 1.5 securities ratios for non-bank deposit taking licensees. The Central Bank also announced its intention of collateralizing loans for up to six months as liquidity support for licensees, if required. This was coupled with a reduction in interest rates on loans from international financial institutions, which included the possibility of extending the IMF Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan by USD10 million, to USD390 million, and disbursing an USD80 million policy-based loan from IIB.

Apart from the recession and the global pandemic, Barbados has experienced two natural hazards in 2021 that have had a significant impact on the country. The first was the eruption of the La Soufrière volcano on St Vincent and the Grenadines, which erupted in April 2021 resulting in damages and losses of approximately BBD$87.1 million from impacts to a majority of the productive sector, for example, in agriculture, fishing, tourism, and manufacturing[10]. The second natural hazard was the passage of Hurricane Elsa on 2 July that resulted in power outages, fallen trees, flash flooding and damaged roads. Whilst there were no reported deaths, it was the first hurricane to make landfall since 2018 when Hurricane Maria hit Barbados. No damages were found but given there was significant disruption to businesses during a year where there already significant disruption to economic activity, it is safe to say that productivity of sector was further stretched.

On 4 June 2020, in response to the worsening economic and fiscal external position, the newly appointed government announced the new BBD$150 million IMF loan aimed at restoring macroeconomic stability. It included the suspension of debt payments, and a comprehensive restructuring of domestic and external debt[11]. The loan was also coupled with an increase in the discount rate from 7% to 9%, and a reduction in the liquidity ratio by 3 percentage points. In addition, Barbados entered into an adjustment program under the IMF’s Extended Fund Facility (EFF) on 20 July 2020[12]. Further details of the EFF can be found at [13].

The World Bank estimates that the impact of COVID-19 in Latin America and the Caribbean. [Page 2–3] [14]

## Level of Professionalism

Despite these setbacks, the Government has spent significantly and is still looking to further invest in building up the water sector's resilience due to the precarious situation of the water infrastructure and its role in supporting economic development. The Government of Barbados has utilized funding from its budgetary allotment (fiscal), the Tourism Enhancement Fund and regional and international funding, including a USAID grant, to build up and upgrade the water sector.

The 3R-CRWBS project is one such initiative undertaken on the part of the GoB to adapt to the impact of climate change. The country is facing a crisis in which water availability to support the economy is going to be increasingly compromised by the global impact of climate change, with food production being expected to be increasingly aggravated through the global impact of climate change on food and supply chains. To counter this, Barbados is seeking to increase food production as an adaptation measure. The 3R-CRWBS project will provide a sustainable solution for water management in Barbados by being found to address current and future water scarcity constraints. At present large volumes of precious water resources are lost through leakage and inefficiencies in the water sector. The country has a linear model of water provision and use has to change to a closed loop through reuse and replenishment.

BIWA, the sole water service authority in Barbados, is critical to guaranteeing the water security of Barbados. However, the current infrastructure is not sufficient to meet future demand for water. In order to guarantee this security, it is expected that its function in the future will not be limited to the production and distribution of water but also demonstrating, raising awareness, and building capacity within localities about the importance of water conservation and management. The 3R-CRWBS project aims at helping BIWA needs to improve its efficiency and build greater resilience to climate variability and change. Here, efficiency refers to the ability to produce more with less while resilience is the ability to adapt to change. This project support the Government of Barbados and BIWA efforts to build resilience in the water sector by closing the cycle of water use and reuse.

The use of reclaimed water is not widespread and there are some reservations by the public to utilise the resource hence the investment in upgrading the plants to generate water to quality reuse must be coupled with building commitment and public education needed to overcome the paradigm shift in behaviour attitude.

The GoB is seeking funding that will facilitate its ability to use as a tool for the implementation of Barbados Green Economy as explained in the Moore (2014) or the Barbados Partnership for Action on Green Economy (PAGE) with the United Nations Environment Programme. To this end, the total estimated capital cost in the short-term is US$358 million and US$679 million in the medium term. The System is US$358 million in the short-term using to US$201 million in the medium term. The Table below captures the relative contributions to capital costs planned for the water sector. Just looking at the capital investments planned, the 3R-CRWBS project will contribute approximately $679 million or 40% and 58%, respectively. At a national scale, GCF's contribution amounts to 22% in the short-term, falling to 16% in the medium term.. Details on the source of capital costs is depicted in Table 3.

## Future expenditure for expansion of the sewer collection system to service Greater Bridgetown—post-2030.

### Expenditure for the expansion of the sewer collection system to service the South Coast area—2025.

The planned capital expenditure for the expansion of the sewer collection systems for the South Coast and Greater Bridgetown would occur after the completion of the upgrades to the two Sewage Treatment Plants. This level of concessional financing, given fiscal space and the programme on which the GOB is embarked upon, will be provided by the World Bank. The project is expected to generate significant benefits in terms of the financial and economic benefits that would flow from the programme. The project risk is being shouldered by the GOB considering that more than 80% of the financing will come from long-term loans. Given the nature of the project, it is imperative for the GOB to ensure that the investment in climate resilient development means that minimising the debt burden is imperative. In this light the level of concessional funding is critical.

The concessionality will benefit the following stakeholders:

- 155 farmers through increased and assured availability of water for irrigation, enabling the expansion of irrigated agriculture for new entrants.
- Communities through improved employment opportunities.
- Local businesses supporting the farming sector through increased economic activity.
- General population through increased food availability and security.

The BWA through the efficient use of scarce resources to support irrigated agriculture and placing addition stress on scarce resources while meeting demand for potable water supply and wastewater treatment.

Visitors to Barbados through the maintenance and improvement of the marine environment and ecosystems.

Visitors and the general population through reduced public health risks associated with deterioration in bathing water quality.

The BWA through improved training and capacity to manage the operation and maintenance of wastewater systems, minimization of the cost of operation and maintenance of the collection systems and wastewater treatment plants, and greater ability to comply to regulatory requirements concerning quality of service and water quality regulations.

The BWA through increased income generation through the sale of treated reclaimed water.

The Government of Barbados through improved economic development opportunities by increasing income by maintaining visitor numbers, increased economic activity and employment in the agriculture sector as a result of the irrigated economy development opportunities.

The general population through increased awareness of the impacts of climate change and incentives encouraging conservation, recycling and reuse.

# Environmental Sustainability

This project has the potential to significantly increase overall environmental sustainability through the following:

1. Producing tertiary quality reclaimed water that is safer for the environment (Activities 1.1 and 1.2). Previous studies by Irvine, Oxford and Suckoo (2021) have demonstrated the positive impact that


---
**Page 53**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

The implementation of centralised wastewater systems has on the marine environment. The high level of treatment and operation to be achieved through the upgrading, training and capacity building will ensure that the two Sewage Treatment Works continue to make a positive contribution.

### i. Subsequent Phases (Activities 3.1.2-3.1.5)

Enhance water quality in the coastal zone by implementing technologies along the west coast for augmenting water supply and protecting the west coast ecosystem (Activity 3.2.1).

* Frequently flushing of the sewer system and treatment works to reduce bacteria (and other agricultural runoff) will safeguard groundwater quantity and improve water quality (Activity 1.3.2).
* Implement a sewer monitoring programme that can reduce or mitigate vulnerabilities from excessive rainfall, which could otherwise result in environmental pollution from "overflowing" of the sewage system (Activity 1.3.1).
* Install a computerized real-time management system (CRTMS) for automated controls and sludge dewatering) that will reduce GHG emissions (all but 0.2.1).
* Promoting water conservation, re-use options (Activities 4.2.2, 4.3.1 and 4.3.2).

In summary, with respect to Circular Economy, the use of reclaimed water provides a positive means of reducing demands on water resources. If this facility is not developed, then demand for water would have to be met through greater groundwater abstraction, depolluting aquifers and enhancing the risk of saline intrusion.

The Government of Barbados through the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security have determined the TDS levels in the water that can be used for irrigation purposes (i.e. 1000 mg/l). The reclaimed water produced at the BSTP with 450 mg/l, Ayers and Westcot in 1985 (See Feasibility Study), has been repeatedly re-referenced for the Water Quality for Agriculture guidance, which indicates that regardless of the nature of the soil or application, a TDS level of 2000 mg/l is considered to be the maximum allowable. The reclaimed water produced at BSTP has a slight to moderate impact on soil that can be managed or addressed. This water quality standard takes into consideration the fact that the reclaimed water will be used for irrigation purposes and not for increasing the efficiency of the irrigation systems. For example, sprinkler systems are not recommended. However, drip irrigation is acceptable provided that the TDS levels in the reclaimed water do not exceed 1000 mg/l as determined by the authorities. This is intended to minimizing the chances of system clogs. As part of the monitoring of water quality process, the following activities are critical:

* Activity 3.2.1: Implement a Remote Concentration Monitoring (RCM) membrane systems to reduce the total dissolved solids concentration of the reclaimed water produced at the BSTP.
* Activity 1.3.1: Implement a sewer monitoring programme that will include the installation of flow measurement devices and sensors, as well as the implementation of measures to prevent stormwater infiltration to the sewer. Mechanisms that identify and reduce or mitigate vulnerabilities in the wastewater system.
* Activity 1.3.2: Establish on-site laboratory facilities and personnel at the BSTP to generate influent and effluent water quality data to inform operations control strategies that optimize operations and reduce operational costs.
* Activity 1.3.3: Implement a Computerized Real-time Management System (CRTMS) at the BSTP to integrate all of the above systems into one comprehensive system.

Activity 1.2.1 is important for bringing down the TDS levels in the water; whereas, Activity 1.3.2 will support real time testing and monitoring of water quality, with protocols for shut off if there are unfavourable conditions. The CRTMS will provide a means to monitor and automate and remote shut-down of the system if need. This include the implementation of meters.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 48

The planning for long-term assessment and monitoring of residual in the soil under irrigation by reclaimed water has already started. The Ministry of Health and Ministry of Agriculture are developing protocol and procedure for monitoring medium to long term changes in soil chemistry. This aspect was discussed during stakeholder consultations with BWA, which were held on 20th June 2017. Monitoring of reclaimed water quality will be carried out firstly by BWA at the plant level (operational standards) and the Ministry of Environment through the Environmental Protection Department. The Ministry of Environment will conduct periodic testing and analyse water and wastewater samples. The Ministry of Environment through the Environment Protection Department, in collaboration with the Ministry of Health and Ministry of Agriculture and Water Security, will monitor the water quality and enforce codes governing usage of this treated wastewater.

### Gender Equality

The issue of gender equality is high on the agenda for this project because issues emerging from the gender analysis show that the fact a gender gap exists within the BWA related to more men being involved in the technical work of the Wastewater Sector Infrastructure and Services and that BWA appears to be underperforming with regard to women’s participation. The project will ensure that women are proportionally represented among other vulnerable groups. The youth and community members will be considered beyond consultation for training as they will form an integral part of the Training and outreach activities, which is expected to increase their awareness on gender equality issues.

The project proposes that a Gender Action Plan (GAP) be prepared in order to address all sectors of the community: users of reclaimed water and potential users of the product or service. The target beneficiaries include women and men, youth and children, as well as vulnerable groups such as people with disabilities and older persons. The project expects the Government component will continue to conform to the BWA’s recruitment policy which ensures desirability of employment, which is also in line with the GCF’s and ADB’s policies and guidelines on gender and social inclusion.

In addition, as an example, the Project will conduct Gender Inclusive Training (GIT) for all staff. The project proposes that a proportionate representation be ensured by having at least 50% of women in the training. The SOEs of the AE and the BWA include provisions for representation of both genders as the Gender Action Plan aims to be congruent with the SOE’s gender action plans. By adopting a gender-sensitive approach in its mandate on climate change, the Fund commits to contributing to gender equality as enshrined in international agreements and national constitutions, and other human rights agreements.

Therefore, the following key activities are earmarked for this project to strengthen gender equality within BWA's operations and the overall management of the water sector in Barbados:

- There will be stakeholder consultations throughout the lifetime of the project that will aim to engage all stakeholders (men and women, youth and children, vulnerable groups) (Activity 1.4.2)
- There will be social and gender safeguards to ensure that women, children and the vulnerable are protected from negative impacts of the project.
- There will be gender sensitive community engagement to ensure that needs of both female and male headed households are incorporated when installing decentralized wastewater treatment systems in the targeted communities within the Gender Gap Zone A (Activity 1.4.1)
- There will be a gender awareness training programme for BWA staff (Activity 3.2.1)
- The updated SOPs and operational manual to include gender and social inclusion considerations, including provisions of the provision of SGBV (Activity 3.2.1)
- The project will also ensure that specific gender equality and limit gender based legislative provisions (Activity 4.1.1)
