## Tax Exemptions
Goods and Services procured with the GCF Proceeds for the implementation of the Project are exempt from eligible taxes and duties according to Article XIX and Article XXVI of the Caribbean Country Climate Change Centre (CCCC) Act, 2015 and Articles 34 and 36 of the Barbados Water Authority Act, 2008. The Caribbean Country Climate Change Centre (CCCC), as a statutory body, also enjoys tax exemptions from eligible taxes and duties according to Article XIX and Article XXVI of the CCCC Act, 2015 establishing the respective entities, the project shall be borne these costs and the Fund shall provide an additional contribution for the payment of taxes during the implementation of the Project.

## Parallel Financing
The BWA is owned and is managed by the BWA, which is a Statutory Body, charged with supplying the island with potable water as well as the provision of wastewater treatment and disposal services to the seaweed areas of Bridgetown. The BWA has been identified in the National Water Resources Assessment, control and protection of the water resources in the public's interest. The project will be implemented by the BWA and is not planned for the SCSTCP. In an effort to mitigate the effects of climate change on the island’s potable and non-potable water supply and increase resilience to climate change and reduce the impact of the primary treated effluent on the

## Capacity Building and Technology Development/Transfer (max 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

### C.3 Does the GCF funding support capacity building activities?

## Capacity Building Activities

Outcome 1 will include targeted capacity building activities under Outcome 3, which will be specialized and customized training for BWA employees to be able to operate, manage and maintain the new systems. This includes training in the operation of the water treatment plant, PV installations, the CEA equipment upgrades, RO technology implemented, decentralized wastewater treatment plants, the upgraded BSTP, monitoring instrumentation and the updated SOPs, and the operational manual and risk management framework (See Activities 3.1.2 and 3.1.3). There will also be training in SCADA, which is currently not available at BWA and will require specific knowledge or expertise to maintain it. Also, there will be a University of the West Indies Gender Seminar for all employees involved with the project, including women, to ensure that gender considerations are taken into account when implementing the project. The training will also cover water sector and gender considerations. Notable is that some of these trainings will also be open to other stakeholders in the water sector who may not have been directly involved in the project but can benefit from the RAFF mechanism. This Project will expand the RAFF to support the Private Sector’s engagement in the water sector, including training for private sector employees on how to use the RAFF and the impact of tertiary reclaimed water. In addition, the RAFF is seen as critical element to engaging the private sector in the water sector, including training for private sector employees on how to use the RAFF and the flow in and out to support activities related to the adoption of decentralized treatment systems as well as dual piping, inter alia.

Under outcome 4, there will be capacity building at two levels: (i) re-education of communities on how to use water more efficiently and best practice for agriculture and other non-potable uses; teachers, students, farmers and businesses about the impact of climate change on water resources and their impact on livelihoods; and (ii) training of BWA staff in the operation and maintenance of direct potable reuse (DPR) and indirect potable reuse (IPR) to build climate resilience (Activity 4.3.1); and (iii) a gender-sensitive approach will be taken when implementing these activities, including training on water resources and the water sector and the role of wastewater treatment and reuse, conservation, recycling etc (Activity 4.3.2). The key difference is that this first capacity building activity will be more structured, such as through workshops, seminars, etc., rather than just a one-time type information programs. See Section B.3 (Project description) for more details on these activities.

The combined budget for Activities 3.1.1, 4.3.1 and 4.3.2 is US$0.3 million.

## Technology Transfer Activities

Majority of outcomes 1 and 2 in this project are heavily focused on technology solutions and the transition to a more sustainable future for the move towards a low-emission and climate resilient pathway water and wastewater sector in Barbados. Outcome 1 is primarily focused on upgrades to wastewater treatment plants, including the BSTP, which will be upgraded to meet the Barbadian government’s proposed reuse water quality standard (v) and simple use of existing infrastructure and process components for the upgrade to the BSTP. Outcome 1 also


---
**Page 60**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 54

includes investments in decision support tools, which include a sewer monitoring programme that is equipped with flow measurement and rain-gauging equipment at both the Bridgetown and South Coast Sewage Treatment Plants (STPs) to inform decision making and climate resilient building (Activity 1.3.1) and a Computerized Real-time Management System (CRRMS) to inform decision making and climate resilient building (Activity 1.3.3). The final output for outcome 1 (output 1.4) is highly relevant to the achievement of GCP’s low emission potential, but the GOB’s goal is to have zero emissions from its STPs by 2050.

The combined budget for Activities 1.1.1, 1.2.1, 1.3.1, 1.3.3, 1.4.1, 4.1.1, 4.2.2, 4.3.2 and all of System 2 is USD$0.9 million.

All of outcome 2 installations (PV, RE) and controls for energy efficiency (EE) and sludge emissions reduction (SRE) and efficient technology transfer, which not only supports the achievement of GCP’s low emission potential, but the GOB’s goal to have zero emissions from its STPs by 2050.

A key element of technology transfer is having the right incentives and legal framework in place. In this regard, activities 4.1.1 (legislative review) and 4.2.2 (incentive programme) can be considered as key.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The section reviews the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## Impact Potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Contribution to the SDG Goals

The 3R-CreAWs project is strictly aligned to the Fund’s strategic vision of promoting the paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development and supporting developing countries, like Barbados, to implement the requirements of the Paris Agreement and the UNFCCC. Overall, the project is anticipated to have an impact on 136,220 direct beneficiaries and indirectly benefit over 457,890 people in Barbados. Given that the country’s economy is heavily reliant upon productive sectors of Barbados – agriculture and tourism; the importance of this project for promoting sustainable development is underscored.

### Mitigation Impact

The total CO₂ impact from this project can avoid about 35,275 tCO₂ over 25 years (See Annex 22 for calculations). The policy and regulatory environment in place in Barbados are currently highly conducive for the use of renewable energy sources. In particular, the Electricity Act No. 10 of 2006, which was amended to include a climate-neutral Barbados is regulated in the Electric Light and Power Act (ELPA), which prescribes feed-in tariffs for renewable energy projects. The project will also contribute towards the country’s goal of increasing the share of renewable energy sources that can benefit from feed-in tariffs while also facilitating a shift to low-emission sustainable development pathway in Barbados. The total CO₂ equivalent emission reduction from this project is estimated at 1,025 tCO₂ over 25 years. This is due to the installation of solar PV panels which will reduce the demand for electricity and thus reduce emissions associated with the upgrades. However, it is also notable that EE will be increased through the installation of solar PV panels as well as other energy efficiency measures. The project will also make use of technology available at the proposal stage, such as characteristics of the sluice, to estimate reduced emissions due to EE; however, the monitoring plan for the project aims to track the changes in EE over the lifespan of the system.

Also notable is that the BSTS is estimated to consume about 4.500 kWh of energy per day which equates to energy costs of US$70,000 per year*. Currently there is a 0.18MW of PV power installed and through this project, an additional 1MW of PV panels will be toted off to set the demands of the upgraded system.

### Adaptation Impact

A few impacts of this project are designed to contribute to increased climate-resilient sustainable development with emphasis on the water and wastewater sector. The following specific details per component of this project should be noted and how they promote climate-resilient sustainable development in Barbados:

- **Component 1** will not only install wastewater treatment technologies that are energy efficient, but also provide a significant improvement to the resilience of the system as a result of the upgraded STP, particularly for hydrometeorological hazards such as rainfall, including severe storms; and sea level rise. The project is expected to benefit approximately 20 communities located in Zone A which will address severe water shortages for approximately 225 households and is considered to be a “good demonstration” of strengthening the adaptive capacities of individuals and businesses to climate risks associated with water availability.


---
**Page 62**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 56

## Component 2 is primarily focused on renewable energy and energy efficiency; however, climate resilience is being promoted in the infrastructure installed, where appropriate, for example the PV systems will utilize solar panels resistant all of at least Category 3 hurricane.

### Component 3: Climate-resilient Wastewater Management

This component aims to support the implementation of climate-responsive wastewater technologies and the use of reclaimed water. This will be achieved through (i) awareness raising on the benefits of wastewater reuse, including its role in risk-reduction processes and (ii) adaptive capacity and reduced exposure to climate risks.

In terms of institutional strengthening, the targeted training for operators will provide awareness of waste water management practices that are climate-resilient. The project team will be mindful of climate risks posed to the upgraded systems and measures to mitigate/manage these risks. This component will also ensure that there is no overlap with other projects that identify any gaps for further strengthening of climate resilience.

The public re-education and awareness raising will also highlight the nexus between water, sanitation, climate change and the role of reclaimed water in this context. It is critical for enhancing knowledge around the use toward reclaim water as the farmers, schools and communities will be engaged through various media such as radio, television, training sessions, structure weekly TV and radio programmes, and social media.

### 3B-Crewit

This project aims to enable a paradigm shift in the wastewater management practices of Barbados that includes the use of low carbon and climate resilient water and energy management technologies and strategies. This will be achieved through the development of a national strategy for sustainable well-being and productivity of Barbadians. Based on the TOC, this will be realized through four outcomes that are expected to be achieved by 2035.

The long-term results (outcomes) of this project will enable the technology transfer required for adaptation to, and employment of climate-resilient wastewater management practices in Barbados. This will lead to transformation through the strengthening of (i) governance and legislative frameworks; (ii) technical capacity building; (iii) institutional capacity building; and (iv) public buy-in and leadership.

For more details at the output and activity level, see Sections B2 (a) and B3.

Achieving this paradigm shift required the project design to place emphasis towards putting processes, structures, stakeholders, knowledge and systems in place that would catalyse scalability, replicability. Technical assistance will be provided by the relevant sectors below demonstrating how the project addresses these areas to increase the paradigm shift potential.

### Scaling-up and replication

In the context of Barbados, the interest in the use of reclaimed water has been documented for some time; for example, the National Water Masterplan for Barbados, which was drafted in 2008, identified the need to encourage reclamation of wastewater as a key recommendation from stakeholder consultations. Further, additional interviews with stakeholders and focus group discussions conducted by the project confirmed that there is still strong support for the use of reclaimed water for non-potable uses. Examples of types of non-potable uses and level of support by interviewees are depicted in the Figure 14 below.


---
**Page 63**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 57

## Figure 14: types of non-usable levels and use level of interviewees

Source: Feasibility Study Report (2021)

Apart from a clear signal of interest, including at the policy level, the demand for reclaimed water from just the perspective of the agriculture sector is high. For instance, the overall estimated volume of reclaimed water available in Barbados is 35 million cubic meters per year, with an operational capacity of 40% (Barbados Water Authority) with a projected 21,900 m³/year by 2030 and notes that if the southern part of Barbados alone, there is at least 7.2 hectares of land eligible for irrigation, and given that the irrigation water needs between 35 to 40% of reclaimed water, this implies a demand of approximately 18 million cubic meters per year, which is more than the required (demand!). The implication of these figures is that the demand for reclaimed water for agricultural purposes in Barbados alone is high. This high demand is testament of the urgent need and high potential for scalability and replicability of this project.

Further, this project's scalability and replicability will be enhanced by specifically targeting a combination of activities targeting private sector engagement (see more details in Section B.3., Output 4.2 and some of Outputs 5.1 and 6.1). The project is also expected to have a positive impact on the operation and maintenance of treatment systems in areas not served by the BSTP or SCSTP. Recalling that these two plants only serve 5% of the population of Barbados, then the scope for scaling this technology is enormous.

Given that there is existing interest in the use of treated wastewater, coupled with the types of activities targeting private sector engagement and the positive impact on the operation and maintenance of treatment systems to advance the application of wastewater treatment technologies, then this project is further considered to have high potential for scaling up without equally increasing the total costs of implementation.

The highly innovative nature of this project provides strong grounds for replication across the Caribbean region. This can be understood from the perspective of the Caribbean in many development areas, in particular, the country's approach to climate change mitigation and adaptation. A critical component of the GCF Programme is the Resilience Programme, which was launched by the GCF Executive Director, Dr. Glemanic. In a recent mission he stated that "We are indeed extremely interested in the Roots to Resilience Programme approach because it could serve as a model to a number of countries in the Caribbean region and beyond." The project is also aligned with the Barbados Climate Change Action Plan (BCCAP) which was launched by the Minister of Environment, Energy and Climate Change, Hon. Dr. Mark Brantley, in 2019.

**Note:** A total of 75 hectares of its is eligible for irrigation in the southern part of the island; however, this project is delivering water to 385 hectares.

# Knowledge Sharing and Learning

A key component of this project has activities to support the creation or strengthening of knowledge, collective learning processes or institutions. Component one will include the installation of sensors in the upgraded STP at BWA (Activity 1.3.2) for monitoring influent and effluent. It will collect data that is important for promoting transparency and accountability in terms of treated wastewater quality and quantity. The project will also develop a database to store this information, which can be used as a resource and the overall impact of this project and beyond its life. There are also important synergies with other projects in the region, such as those related to climate change and water resources, and climate-related risks that can impact on the wastewater system. The resulting datasets from the various monitoring systems will not only address a single data gap but exists for the BWA, but more importantly, it will be used by other stakeholders in the region.

The project will also develop a peer exchange platform (PEP) to share lessons and best practices within the CARICOM region. This will include sharing of lessons learned from the implementation of this project as well as sharing of lessons and best practices.


## Creating an Enabling Environment

Creating an enabling environment for this project will have long-lasting effects on the sustainability of outcomes and results beyond completion of the intervention. Under component three, investments in technical capacity building are critical to ensure that the wastewater system is sustainable after the project has been implemented. This is why customized training certification will be developed by this project in collaboration with academic institutions for access by all BWA employees with considerations for gender equality. The development and implementation of a comprehensive training program for highly trained operators at the BWA will ensure that there is a well-developed preventative maintenance (PM) program to extend the life of the equipment and to keep it functioning at the design level to maintain effluent quality.

This is an arrangement between CARICOM Member States for the provision of a high-strength economic space, open through the removal of restrictions resulting in free movement of goods, services, persons, capital and technology.


---
**Page 65**
---

quality and reduce any environmental impacts or health and safety issues. Majority of component four is designed to strengthen the enabling environment. Specifically, the outputs will ensure that there is awareness of the up-to-date legislation, mechanisms are in place bolstering private sector engagement in the water sector, a national climate change adaptation strategy has been developed, a national disaster risk reduction strategy, incentive programme, expanding the RAFF and targeted PEA materials). All of these activities are complementary to component four. The overall objective is that the capacity of the water sector and its financial capacity would be strengthened to sustain and scale up the use of wastewater technologies and reclaimed water in Barbados.


Regulatory Framework and Policies

The Government, as an indication of their commitment to advancing water security in Barbados, has updated several legislations (See details in Section B.3., Output 4.1). The aim of this project is to review them and outline a roadmap for the implementation of these laws. This will be done through a series of workshops, particularly as it relates to ensuring sustainability of the results of this project. Further, a water and sanitation master plan will be developed, which is a strategic planning document that will give life to the recently updated legislation. The development of such a plan would help to strengthen the overall regulatory and policy frameworks governing wastewater in Barbados.


Contribution to National Climate-Resilient Development Pathways

Component two of this project strongly supports the achievement of the Barbados National Energy Policy (BNEP) which aims at achieving 100% renewable energy and zero emissions by 2030. Centralized wastewater management relies on expensive high-emission electricity supplied from conventional power plants that use fossil resources, which not only increases operational costs but also contributes to global warming.


This project is also in support of the achievement of the RZRP. The RZRP provides the overarching framework that allows the integrated approach to addressing the negative impacts of climate change. The RZRP has been aligned with the Barbados National Development Plan (BNDP) and the national programme for Barbados. The primary focus is on improving the social and environmental circumstances of the population, while ensuring economic growth and development. The implementation of the RZRP is hinged on six thematic areas: Shelter, Water, Energy, Waste, Land use, and Ecosystems Management. This project is in alignment with 4 (Water, Energy, Waste and Ecosystems Management) of the 6 thematic areas. Notwithstanding the themes under which the RZRP are also aligned to Barbados’ Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) priority areas.

## Design and prioritization of water reuse strategies

In addition to the foregoing, the impact potential of this project from an environmental, social, economic and gender-sensitive development perspective is highlighted below.

### Environmental co-benefits
- This project can avoid approximately 35,275 ICOut over the estimated 25 years' lifespan of the PV system.
- Improved testing capacities at BWA will ensure efficient meet environmental standards for use by agricultural users and other water-reliant sectors.
- Improved air quality in the areas proximate to the enhanced systems to deal with effluents.
- Reduced groundwater contamination at the sites within Zone A benefiting from decentralized wastewater treatment systems.
- Increased potable water availability by using the reclaimed water to replenish aquifers thereby creating a greater amount of potable water and increasing water security through indirect potable reuse. Reduced wastewater runoff and nutrient loading in the marine environment.

### Economic co-benefits

#### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

##### Vulnerability of Barbados

Barbados is one of the Small Island Developing States (SIDS) that makes it vulnerable to the effects of climate change and other extreme hydro-meteorological events. These characteristics are exacerbated by its unique geography, which includes a high proportion of infrastructure in narrow coastal areas, restricted economic base and reliance on natural resources combined with limited land area. The island has been identified as one of the most vulnerable SIDS due to 20% of Barbados’s critical infrastructure, population and economic activity is located within the coastal zone. Specifically, over 60% of the island's total population is located in the three coastal parishes of St. James, Saint Peter and Saint Philip. The vulnerability of Barbados is further exacerbated by its reliance on climate and critical infrastructure to sea level rise, storm surge and inundation as is high a result of its location.

##### Environmental risks

Barbados faces the adverse effects of climate change and natural hazards, with their cumulative impacts being felt across all sectors of society. These include increased frequency of weather events and slow onset events impacting national income flows, increasing indebtedness and vulnerability to debt service payments, as well as a decline in tourism revenue. The modified environment as well as the social and economic stability of the country, Barbados considers climate change to be one of its most pressing challenges. This is due to the fact that it has an important supply function of demographic and socio-economic inputs; this country is among those Caribbean nations most vulnerable to climate change. Further, there are factors unique to the country that add to or further complicate these vulnerabilities.

- Water scarcity as a result of Barbados’s unique hydrogeological
- Rapid urbanization and population growth compared with other SIDS in the region, which has led to a highly modified natural environment, unsustainable development practices in the past, loss of ecosystem services, lack of green spaces, ageing infrastructure and housing stock, among others,

## Social and economic risks: Climate change will impact already vulnerable groups disproportionately, including youth and women, as well as lower income communities. Alongside the direct environmental effects of climate change, such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, there are a range of social and economic impacts.

### Health:
- Increased heat stress
- Greater prevalence of water and vector-borne diseases

### Tourism: 
- Damage to coastal tourism infrastructure, biodiversity, and landscape. Any negative impact on the tourism sector will have significant implications for the economy and competition.

### Water resources:
- Reduced freshwater availability for the population of Barbados resulting from drought or groundwater contamination from flooding, soil or pollutant intrusion or saline intrusion.
- Fishery and agricultural industries: loss of domestic and/or international competitiveness resulting from drought, reduced water quality, saline intrusion, and poor and massive species outbreaks and spread, and ecosystem destruction.

This project directly addresses the risks of natural resource scarcity, which are exacerbated by climate change. Barbados is particularly vulnerable to associated climate change in Barbados as it faces a dilemma currently being faced by all persons in Barbados, albeit the vulnerable groups that this project will address include farmers. The island's drinking water supply is used extensively by small farmers as they attempt to grow crops and livestock. Farmers are also heavily dependent on rainwater for irrigation purposes since the government offers rebate incentives for the use of sprinkler and drip irrigation systems but there is no incentive or support provided for the use of rainwater harvesting systems. The island's agricultural sector uses the use of garden hose-feeding and hand-watering, and there is little reuse of wastewater for irrigation. It should be noted that the majority of the agricultural land in Barbados is owned by a small percentage, comprise major of the labour force involved in land preparation, weeding, crop and protection planting.

## Economic and Social Development Level of the Country:
The World Bank's 2019 Living Conditions 2018/2019 found that overall levels of poverty were rising, up from 15% in 2010 to 17%. A further 11% of the population was considered to be vulnerable. The Survey estimated that 21% of women were poor while only 14% of men were poor. In 2019, the poverty rate for households headed by a woman was more than double those headed by a man. The poverty and vulnerability stems both from direct and indirect discrimination, in the workplace and in social capital. Women are disproportionately represented among the unemployed and those working in low-paying jobs in poverty. The child poverty rate was nearly double for those households in which females were the main income earners compared to those where males were the main income earners.

The overall unemployment rate is estimated to be around 10%, with female unemployment rate at 8% and men's unemployment rate at 7%. In addition, the survey found that only about one-third of employed women would work more hours, compared to a 1.6% of employed men. Unpaid family workers tend to be exclusively female". This situation has been exacerbated since the onset of the COVID pandemic, as employment rates for women fell by almost 20%, while those for men fell by only about 5%. The survey found that almost half of respondents who were employed before the pandemic reported losing their job (IDB 2020). Not surprisingly, the survey also found that women are more likely to be in low-paying jobs (41%) compared to middle and high-income ones (40% and 28%, respectively). The adverse fiscal and GDP impact of the pandemic was significant. While the government achieved the largest primary surplus in its history at 6% of GDP for FY 2020/2021, the country's real public spending contracted by 6.2%, and real economic activity contracted by some 18% during 2020 and 3% in the first quarter of 2021, as the

sharp decline in tourism negatively impacted the economy as a whole[1]. With a global economic recession extending well into 2021, the outlook is negative.

### D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The GOB ratified the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 1994 and the Kyoto Protocol in 2000. In September 2015, Barbados communicated its Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC) to the UNFCCC[3]. The Paris Agreement was adopted at COP21 in December 2015; Barbados ratified it on 4 November 2016. The ambitions of the Paris Agreement to limit global warming to well below 2°C and pursue efforts to limit temperature increase to 1.5°C are ambitious, but the overall level of ambition in 2015 INDCs would be too low[4]. Notwithstanding Barbados' historically low level of responsibility for the increase of greenhouse gas concentrations in the atmosphere, the GOB, in its 2019 Barbados National Climate Change Strategy (BNCCS), committed to reducing its emissions intensity by at least 65% from a fossil fuel-free economy and to reduce GHG emissions across all sectors to as close to zero as possible by 2030.

In the area of climate change adaptation, Barbados maintains several important legal frameworks. Broadly speaking, adaptation in Barbados is framed within the following legal tools: The Proclamation of the Planning and Development Commission Act (PDC) 1976; The National Environment Management Act (NEMA) 2003; and considerations for the first time in legislation and has been already approved by the Cabinet. The 2021 PDP addresses the critical impacts of climate change on Barbados through policies and strategies that enable the people to thrive in a changing environment[5]. The National Climate Change Strategy (NCCS) 2019-2030[6] operationalizes the PDP and provides the vehicle through which public investment will be directed. R2P2 objectives are:

* **Low- and middle-income homes more resilient to extreme weather events as well as possible loss of the electricity grid and potable water distribution systems,** and
* **Increase freshwater storage capacity and water use efficiency.**

The Ministry of Environment, Water Resources and Energy (MEWR) and the BWA in collaboration with the Government of Barbados (GOB) set out proposals for changes to the Zoning taking into account the emerging threats, proposing changes to the zoning and requirements for treatment of wastewaters. The Green Paper marks a significant shift from the previous policy of prohibiting such uses as the primary means of wastewater treatment; 2) development of communal sanitation systems in rural areas; and 3) the use of treated effluent for irrigation purposes. In response, the Government of Barbados (GOB) enacted an National Water Reuse Policy (NWRP), which states that "the introduction of the term 'water must expand beyond conventional means of sources to encompass treated wastewater from municipal and industrial sources as part of the national water resources". These policies coupled with significant investment that the Government of Barbados has made in recent years, demonstrates a clear commitment by the Government of commitment to seeing a paradigm shift in the how reclaimed water is managed and utilized.

The following are relevant legislation and policies from the water sector perspective: The Barbados Water Authority Act (1980): establishes the Barbados Water Authority; Underground Water Control Act (1953): provides for the control and use of the underground sources of water supply in the island. It is unlawful to extract or pump any water from an underground source without a permit issued by the Minister of Water and for the obstruction of underground water. Health Services Act (1968): allows the Minister to divide Barbados into health and sanitation districts. The Minister has the power to construct sewers. This Act also prohibits

taking water from public taps without permission. Irrigation Act (1963); allows the Chief of Agriculture to carry out surveys, investigation or research for irrigation purposes, to distribute water and to control or operate watermeters; Prevention of Floods Act (1951); makes provision for measures for the prevention of floods; National Water Policy (2004); sets out a framework for sustainable development in Barbados through reduction and universal metering and short-term measures such as temporary shutdowns of parts of the system. The policy also includes long-term measures such as the construction of dams and reservoirs; Groundwater Zoning Policy: implemented through the Town and Country Planning Office in collaboration with the Water Authority, this policy is designed to protect groundwater resources and promote a way of managing groundwater resources (2008).Most recently, the BWIA established a WasteWater Department (WWD) to properly manage Barbados' wastewater problems, including building resilience into the county's wastewater system. The National Water Policy (2004) states that water is a resource which must be managed and that water is a national resource which shall be used to improve the quality of life for its citizens, maintain the natural environment and promote sustainable development in Barbados towards achieving social equity, economic development and a green economy. The policy objective is to promote the sale of reclaimed, storm and non-pollute water in urban, agricultural and the industrial sectors such that human health and environmental quality are protected. The Barbados Sustainable Development Policy (2004) sets out the landscape for sustainable development from a Barbadian perspective. Embedded within the Policy are the adopted sustainable development goals and targets. The policy promotes the principles of sustainability, equity and participation. In the context of water resources management, the policy sought to conserve available freshwater and within the agricultural sectors it encouraged the use of organic fertilizers and promoted rainwater harvesting.

The National Strategic Plan of Barbados 2006-2025 recognises that protecting and preserving our natural and built environment is vital to achieving sustainable development. Goal four of the document sought to promote a more efficient, effective and equitable use of water resources through the provision of reliable water supply, and the wise management of our non-renewable natural resources. The strategic plan proposes a series of actions aimed at ensuring that Barbados has access to greater quantities of clean water.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 70

The document discusses the financial feasibility of a project aimed at improving water management systems for reuse and irrigation. It highlights that the onward provision for treated wastewater is critical to generating income, offsetting capital and operational costs, and achieving positive Net Present Value (NPV) ratios as more water is collected and used for irrigation.

### Key Points:

- **Benefit-Cost Ratios**: These become positive with increased water collection and use.
- **Ratio of Water Available for the Business-as-usual Case**: This ratio continues to increase as more water becomes available, indicating a favorable financial outlook.
- **Sensitivity Analysis**: The financial outcome is sensitive to assumptions about irrigation water tariffs. However, this sensitivity decreases due to lower liability substitution and income effects (Hicksian substitution).
- **Economic Activity Impact**: Increases in tariffs might affect the level of economic activity for irrigation farmers.

### Summary:
The DFMED found that the project will be financially sustainable given the investments proposed. The income revenue streams will exceed capital and operational expenses, making it a viable investment. The project also supports institutional strengthening and capacity building within the BWA (Bolivian Water Authority), enhancing planning and management capabilities. It aligns with the goals of the National Climate Change Strategy and the proposed implementation of Innovations Division and Strategic Planning and Regulation Division.

### Use of Best Practices and Available Technologies:
Innovative technologies, such as decentralized sewage collection and treatment sites with clusters in sensitive groundwater extraction zones (Zone A), are being implemented to ensure water quality is maintained while reducing environmental costs. These systems also help mitigate the impact of climate change on groundwater resources and provide additional water resources during droughts.

### Implementation:
- **Final Upgrade Selection**: Criteria included land area requirement, operator skill level, technology adaptability, capital cost, operating labor cost, energy requirements, and maintenance cost.
- **Project Benefits**: The project will benefit the BWA through institutional strengthening and capacity building. It supports the National Climate Change Strategy and aligns with the proposed implementation of Innovations Division and Strategic Planning and Regulation Division.

### Additional Measures:
- **Private Sector Engagement**: Promoting both climate change adaptation and mitigation benefits, including engagement strategies such as RAFF (Rural Adaptation Finance Facility) mechanisms.

The operational systems are manual. There are no functional automated control systems

Sustainability
Paradigm shift in the use of reclaimed water among communities in Barbados (demand side) is a major challenge to be sustained in the medium term. This is due to the expansion of the urban area, which will create capacity and the creation of new demand for wastewater sector (supply side).

The project will make a significant contribution towards achievement of Sustainable Development Goals, specifically targets 2, 6, 7, 8, 11, 13, 17

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for wastewater management in a country-driven manner | Policy, laws and Legislation - the legal framework for wastewater management in Barbados has been rocky. The country is still not able to better inform wastewater management on issues of quality and standards. These include the Zoning Policy and Development Act. | medium | High degree to which the GCF investment contributes to new and improved instruments to achieve sustainable wastewater practices. The Tourism Authority (2002), states that an expenditure in improving the wastewater system would allow a tax credit of 25% on the capital cost of all wells, pipes and pumps used in the treatment and disposal of the wastewater system. The legal and regulatory environment | The overall aim of the project's emphasis is the legislative framework to be put into place which will promote what is now being established as an important sector, as well as (ii) strengthening any gaps in the existing legislation (Activity 4.1.1). Hence, this project will play a key role in strengthening the legislative framework. Further, the project will contribute towards the long-term water and sanitation master plan that will be developed to guide best practice and takes into consideration the new and improved water and waste water policies which are currently being developed for water and sanitation sectors as well as other related expenditure projects including the 30% scheme when approved. Hence, this project provides a comprehensive planning approach to the country's wastewater and sanitation sector. There will be a high assumption that the project's contribution to building technical capacity in the areas of integrated Wastewater (ICAW), operator, CAS, RO etc., will enhance operational effectiveness | National |

expenditure in improving the wastewater disposal system allowed a tax credit of 50% of the capital cost of fittings, pipes and other good used in the improvement of the waste water system.

Yet, a very small number of businesses and/or local communities in the 18 Zones have offices decentralized wastewater treatment systems. There are limited PEA campaigns for wastewater technologies and there are no incentives as well as any policy or financial and incentive frameworks in place.


contributes to significant savings from use of renewable energy which provides contributions for the expansion of a microgrid mechanism that will turn be used to support local communities (households and businesses) in the adoption of wastewater treatment and reuse technologies.

The engagement of the private sector is key to success of this project. There will be a strategic plan for the engagement of the private sector in the provision of wastewater treatment services, including the expansion along the west coast corridor of the installation of decentralized wastewater treatment systems (output 4.2) and the utilization of wastewater by-products (organic and inorganic fertilizers). Further, there will be more incentives to private sector through the development of an incentive framework (output 4.2) and the expansion of the RAPP (Action Plan 4.3).

Market demand is further strengthened through the creation of an enabling business friendly environment. This project's investment in the technical capacities of wastewater personnel (Action Plan 4.1) will engage public and targeted stakeholders (businesses, schools, etc.) to promote for the use of reclaimed water (output 4.3).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 85

## Core indicator B: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards

### Knowledge generation and learning is currently limited at the BWIA due to lack of institutional capacities and to some extent external technical capacities. For example: vital infrastructure such as dams was destroyed by fire in the past and has not been updated to historical flow rates or raw treated water consumption, preventative measures are lacking, there is no laboratory for testing water quality, etc.

### High degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation processes and use of good practices, methodologies and standards

#### These fundamental data sets would be imperative in order to understand the baseline situation of the wastewater sector and to inform the use of appropriate technologies and setting standards.

businesses and the general public,
including tourism on the impact of climate change on water resources and the need to improve water quality (availability as well as the improvement of wastewater policies and indirect potable reuse (IPR) to build resilience in the Water Sector. There will also be a dedicated project officer with an associated knowledge management strategy to collect, disseminate information and promote learning or awareness raising activities by the various outputs of the project.

In terms of the use of good practices, methods and lessons learned, the feasibility studies that informed the design of the project are based on international best practices and standards for the technologies being implemented.

Further, the highly innovative nature of the project, which is being implemented and the urgent need to address climate change risks for the water sector, as demonstrated in the rationale (Section B1), makes the learning potential of this project in identifying good practices and standards critical for the project. ## E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Project M&E Arrangements
Indicators are used in an M&E system's practical applicability as they serve to help practitioners know when outcomes or results have or have not been achieved and inform adjustments to current interventions and decision making toward future interventions. The performance of the 3R-CRWWS project will be monitored using a set of indicators that are defined by the data source, data collection tools, frequency, and budget requirements for monitoring all the indicators of the project. The detailed logframe for the project contains the baselines and targets associated with the indicators. Collectively these tools (M&E Plan and Logframe) will provide the guidance required to monitor the project's progress against its objectives. Monitoring is an ongoing process that will be conducted on a regular basis, and will be reported with the M&E responsibilities given that this project spans a wide cross section of direct beneficiaries and stakeholders including communities, farmers, local authorities and businesses and businesses. Hence, consultations and follow-up with them on the quality of products or services developed and lessons learned will be extensive.

Two types of evaluations will be undertaken during the lifetime of the 3R-CRWWS project – formative and summative evaluation. Notable is that a key requirement of the GCF is that an assessment of the level of achievement of the paradigm shift potential be undertaken during these stages of the project, with the results being reported to the GCF by the implementing agency.

Formative evaluation is generally undertaken during a project's implementation for trying to understand why a program works or doesn't and what other factors (internal and external) are at work during a project life to improve the project's design and chance of successfully achieving the desired outcomes. The formative evaluation will complement the final summative evaluation. The summative evaluation of 3R-CRWWS will use a balance of quantitative and qualitative methods in order to get a better understanding regarding what achieved, how and why. There will also be emphasis on unintended consequences, the evaluation drivers of accountability and will be more outcome-focused than process focused.

CCCCC-GCF Reporting Arrangement


---
**Page 122**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 116

CCCCC reports to the GCF fund will be guided by GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework for Accredited Entities, its Accreditation Master Agreement and terms to be agreed in the funding agreement. Aligned with GCF's Monitoring and Accountability Framework for Accredited Entities, CCCCC will undertake continuous monitoring of the project through site visits and quarterly reports, which will feed into semi-annual performance reports and an annual performance report.

The Annual Performance Reports (APRs) will include the financial management reports and progress report on all activities. It will identify potential risks in project implementation (and mitigation actions). These reports will also evaluate the entities against the targets set out in the logical framework, which are in accordance with the GCF investment criteria. CCCCC monitoring and reporting will consider feedback from direct beneficiaries, communities, stakeholders, and government agencies. This approach allows for a three-level view of information that is not only important to monitor their performance but also products and services they deliver. The APRs will be designed to ensure that buy-in and ownership for relevant products and services are being built at the national level for sustainability, replicability, and scalability purposes.

At the mid-point of the project the formative evaluation report will be submitted to the GCF for review. There will also be a final report at the end of the period of implementation, which will provide a holistic view of the achievements of the project, impact, effectiveness, and efficiency (financial and economic), financial records, stakeholder feedback and lessons learnt. This report will also be accompanied by the final project financial audit to be performed in accordance with GCF's Financial Management Rules and Procedures. The final report will be submitted not later than 15th November of the year following that of completion of the project. CCCCC’s Accreditation Master Agreement. The CCCCC will submit in English the final project report, including the final evaluation report, to the GCF within 6 months of the completion of the project.

BWA-CCCCC Reporting Arrangements

To ensure that the project is implemented in a timely and sustainable, detailed timely monitoring, reporting and evaluation will be carried out. The BWA through the project implementation unit, will provide CCCCC with (a) quarterly progress reports in a format consistent with CCCCC project management requirements; (b) mid-term review report on the status of the project against the indicator’s performance targets, (c) key implementation issues, solutions and lessons; (d) status of implementation of the ESMP (d) updated procurement plan and (e) updated implementation plan for next 12 months; and (f) a project completion report within 6 months of physical completion of the Project. The BWA will submit all reports to CCCCC within 30 days from receipt.

The BWA will submit a project completion report to Government of Barbados and CCCCC.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Technical and operational measures (see note 3, page 4)

As part of output 3.1 (activity 3.1.3), a comprehensive technical and operational risk assessment, including climate change risks, will be undertaken as a central element of the paradigm shift and capacity of the investment. This risk assessment will be conducted by the project management team in its role as project management capacity. An overview of this output can be found in relevant sections of this document (e.g., B.2 and B.3) with details provided in the Feasibility Study (Annex 2). In particular includes a detailed list of technical and operational risks, including climate change risks. The risk assessment will provide an overall summary of the technical and operational risks that will be provided, with an emphasis on risks associated with project management and implementation.

| Selected Risk Factor 1 | Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

## Description

Wastewater treatment is a crucial public service that needs to be available at all times. The introduction of new infrastructural facilities for the treatment of wastewater carries risks associated with interruptions during construction, starting up the new procedures, and integrating them into existing systems.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

BWA staff are very familiar with the current facilities at the WTPs that will see facility upgrades. They will be equipped to manage these changes in a manner similar to how they have managed other changes over time since the implementation of the works. The supplies of the new facilities will be required to install and start operation of all facilities, fine-tune installations onto an initial operating period, as well as training of BWA staff.

Infrastructure works will be undertaken during the season when there is relatively little flow going through the system. This means that the capacity of the existing infrastructure can be used more efficiently. The capacity of the system will be utilized to buffer flows during any planned disruptions (such as disconnecting and reconnecting pipes between existing systems, such as stormwater entry points) and for new modifications to be added to the existing facilities; such planned disruptions are expected to be few. In the event that unexpected disruptions do occur, possibly due to external events, ocean outfall of raw sewage may be required as a solution of last resort but it is not expected that this will be necessary.

Further details on the facilities to be installed and their integration with existing facilities can be found in Annex 2.


## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

## Description

Climate change forecasts indicate a reduction of rainfall of about 9% by 2050 compared to current rainfall. The incidence of severe storms with high rainfall events, however, is expected to increase. Such storms have the potential to overwhelm the wastewater treatment system due to runoff of rain entering the sewerage system.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The sewerage system in Barbados is designed only to convey wastewater but storm water enters the sewerage system during episodes of high rainfall nonetheless. Two main sources of stormwater entry have been identified: (1) manhole covers of the sewerage system are lifted by local neighbours to alleviate surface levels


---
**Page 125**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 119

flooding and is contrary to the building code, several larger residential buildings have connected downspouts to the sewerage connection.

The project includes activities under output 1.3 to (1) identify areas where excessively large inflows generate from and to notify non-compliant building owners of their violation of the building code with an instruction with an instruction to remediate the situation; and (2) to remove or restrict operating access to the manhole covers.

If ocean fall-out of raw sewage occurs, the impacts are mitigated due to the fact that the concentration of waste inorganic solids is low and the large volume of stormwater in the effluent.

## Selected Risk Factor

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

### Discussion

Geologically speaking, Barbados is a limestone dome, largely formed from coral reefs over millennia. As a result, the subsoil is relatively porous due to significant karstification. Infiltration of tertiary effluent fluent from waste treatment plants (WTPs) can pass through cracks and caves, rather than permeate slowly through the soil, which could lead to contamination of the aquifer.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Barbados has a world-class geological setting with emphasis on the porosity of the limestone formations. This is driven by the importance of the limestone formation for its water supply of Barbados – being the only reservoir of freshwater on the island – as well as for the structural integrity of all buildings and infrastructure (mapping of sink holes).

### Figure 16: Typical karstic sub-soil in Barbados. Source: <https://climatechangebarbados.com/water-resources>

The effluent from the WTPs is regularly tested to ensure compliance with regulations, particularly in relation to bio-safety and dissolved nutrients. The infiltration zones for the treated wastewater will be selected on the basis of expert knowledge of the limestone formation in BWIA and other relevant government agencies. Given that the Environmental Protection Department (EPA) regular tests on the water quality to ensure that it meets World Health Organization standards for drinking, any contamination will be observed quickly and addressed immediately.

### Description

In general, Barbados has a well-developed regulatory system for natural environments, built-up areas, economic activities (such as tourism), infrastructure, and others. Compliance and enforcements, however, are not always effective.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Regulatory gaps will be addressed through the project and BWA will be supplied with monitoring tools to support enforcement of regulations.

### Behavioural changes, such as on water use and misuse of the sewerage system in the general population and government staff will take time to materialize. The project has several activities focused on sensitization and awareness-raising. In combination with a growing awareness of the impacts of climate change on Barbados' natural resources and infrastructure, it is expected that the irrational use of water and the sewerage infrastructure will reduce over time.

Mitigation Measure(s): All international services will be procured in USD; hence there are no forex risks. The Barbadian Dollar is pegged to the USD at a rate of 1USD = 2BBD and has been stable for at least 10 years – domestically procured goods will be quoted in BBD.

Procurement of goods for the facility upgrades at the WTPs, however, will be internationally tendered and may be subject to price fluctuations when specified in a currency other than USD. G. OCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

Initial screening of the project activities indicates that the project has an ESS category B rating. This is because while construction is taking place, it is an expansion of existing facilities and without generating significant new emissions or other environmental impacts. The project does not involve the development of assets for export. Nevertheless, and in compliance with government regulations, a full ESIA and an associated ESMP have been carried out as part of this funding proposal.

The ESIA and ESMP are annexed to this document (Annex G1) and comply with the OCF Environmental and Social Guidelines and Social Safeguards Policy and Performance Standards (2015) of CCCC.

The ESIA reports on the area that comprises the project intervention areas, as well as their surrounding areas, specifically in relation to the following components: Direct, Indirect and Influential. Direct and indirect influence areas were defined for most of the project components. The general direct influence includes the area for the construction of the main project activities (such as the expansion of existing facilities) and other structures (such as construction sites and temporary support structures). Indirect influence areas correspond to a buffer surrounding these areas, which may be affected by the implementation of the project activities or during the exploration phase. The extent of this influence area will vary.

Most of the proposed impacts are likely to occur during the construction phase due to construction activities (and clearance), trenching and excavation, operation of heavy machinery, truck traffic, among others. These are likely to cause temporary to short-term negative impacts such as soil erosion, pollution (noise, dust, etc.), alteration of vegetation cover, and disruption of wildlife habitats. The majority of these impacts will be for most of these impacts, meaning that most of them should be of low significance after adequate action is taken during the construction phase. These actions include: revegetation; restoration of disturbed areas; alteration / destruction of terrestrial habitats and traffic disruption and congestion. The alteration / destruction of aquatic habitats (such as fish passage barriers, water quality degradation in the aquifer recharge wells and service areas) are affected. Traffic disruption and congestion will occur due to construction activities and heavy machinery operations. These impacts will be mitigated by the use of heavy machinery circulation, in addition to works occurring next to roads (pipe laying). The 3 items in the ESMP contain mitigation measures for these two moderate impacts, as well as for a host of other minor impacts.

The operational phase will bring the major expected positive impacts such as increased rail services in different areas; reduction of road traffic congestion effects; reduction of pollution problems potentially affecting groundwater quality; increase quantity and quality of groundwater resources in the St. Thomas aquifer recharge facility; improvement of water supply to the population; improvement of water quality in Caribay Bay; improvement of air quality and reduced GHG emissions; energy recovery from waste; employment opportunities; improved conditions for the agricultural sector and increased water availability; reduction of road traffic congestion effects; improved planning; employment opportunities; improved conditions for the agricultural sector and increased water availability; reduction of road traffic congestion effects; increased attraction of tourism, improved public health; education of the general public and awareness.

Avoiding negative impacts during the operational phase requires diligent and efficient facility management, including maintenance. The ESIA has identified a number of O&M activities that will be developed and applies operation and maintenance (O&M) plans, which will be annexed to include the facilities implemented under the project. Elements to be covered in the O&M plans include monitoring of groundwater; BSTP infrastructure; water quality; and other environmental impacts. These O&M plans are included on periodic reports delivered to the competent government agencies (and CZA) in the case of the coral reef environment.

Overall, the ESIA did not identify any significant negative environmental and social impacts from the implementation of the project, specifically due to the upgrade of the Bridgetown BSTP, the implementation of the aquifer recharge facility, the reclaimed water pipeline and the two onsite decentralized treatment plants and associated infrastructure. The ESIA has identified a number of potential negative impacts that have been identified in the construction and operation phases, but these can be adequately mitigated by the

## Stakeholder consultations and grievance redress

Grievance redress is a critical component of this project for both BWA and CCCCC, and it is intended that the project will have an effective mechanism to address grievances. It is known that projects having strong grievance redress mechanism are usually less exposed to reputational risk, less disruptions and more importantly it increases in buy-in and ownerships for projects. Not only is a GRM important for those affected by the project but also for BWA and CCCCC as well as other stakeholders such as the Government of Barbados (GoB) and IE to hear the complaints as it relates to the nature and extent of grievances. It clearly articulates the process through which anyone being affected by the project can submit a grievance and is confident that it


---
**Page 130**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 124

will be addressed in a systematic, fair, transparent and timely manner. Furthermore, it will seek to ensure that the complainant is protected, in so far as to prevent any change of victimization. Operationalization of the grievance or complaints mechanism will be layered in this regard. There will be three levels offered by the Centre for redress: first level will be secured by the AE; and the third by the lender. In the community communication strategy information will be shared on what are grievances and the avenues available to the communities for their voices when needed.

The CCCDD is in ESAP9 outlines considerations for grievance redress. The centre allows for all complaints, concerns, queries, and clarifications regarding projects programmed supported by the Centre can be lodged with the AE or the PSC. These can also be made through the email address at info@cccdd.org.bw or on the following website link: http://www.caribbeanclimateaccount.cbd.int/cccdd/esap9complaints. Project will be monitored during implementation and will proactively check on the risk that were identified at the project development stage. The Centre will also ensure that complainants are provided with a safe, secure and confidential avenues even further by allowing complainants to lodge their grievance in another format where they are comfortable.

This proposed project has been developed through extensive consultations with all stakeholders involved. Consultations have taken place with, inter alia:

- Government agencies that are mandated in fields where water management is relevant, including infrastructure, agriculture, energy, health, environment and environmental health.
- Private sector entities that use and/or discharge large quantities of water.
- University of the West Indies, gender related issues.
- Caribbean Institute for Meteorology and Hydrology (CIMH) for related agencies such as the Caribbean Institute for Meteorology and Hydrology.
- Farmers who are willing to use reclaimed wastewater for irrigation of crops.
- General population, on their willingness to consume agricultural produce grown with reclaimed wastewater.

A full account of stakeholder consultations and a stakeholder management and engagement plan for the implementation of the project is included in Annex 7.

Barbados has an Office of the Ombudsman, established by Act of Parliament in 2009. All government agencies are required to provide written responses to complaints by the Ombudsman on response to complaints from members of the public after having received a written complaint. This is the principal grievance redress mechanism for Barbados. The Centre will not be establishing an alternative to this arrangement. However, the project will establish an internal grievance redress mechanism managed by the PSC as a first-level mechanism to address complaints and concerns from all stakeholders.

Disclosure of the applicable safeguards reports will follow the requirements of the GCF Information Disclosure Policy on Environmental and Social Policy.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 125

## Challenges Facing Barbados and the BWA as an Institution.

The Gender Analysis Process Model used to conduct the gender equality assessment comprised of four main parts, namely: Gender Screening; Gender-Related Data Collection; Gender Impact Assessment and Mainstreaming Gender in the Project Cycle in conjunction with the GCF standards and the COCOCC Gender Policy. Key findings from these four phases are that:

1. **Barbados has made significant human development gains**, but results are not always translated into parliamentary, a 14% gender gap among, and a 60% gender gap among the Cabinet Ministers.
2. **Import to the Barbadian economy is dominated by women**.
3. **There still remains gender segregation of roles, except in technical professions and among legislators**, where there are no women.
4. **The BWA has more men involved in the technical aspects of the Wastewater Division management role than women**.

5. **There are many issues where both men and women share similar views on wastewater collection and treatment among the stakeholders in Barbados**. However, man seeming more concerned with pricing by the public.
6. **Men and Women in Barbados are concerned that climate change will affect the quality of drinking water**, but women seem more concerned than men about this issue.

7. **It is necessary for the BWA Board of Directors to adopt appropriate policies for maintaining Project standards** to ensure gender issues are considered and to guide the decisions made and actions taken by the BWA in all aspects of its operations, including project implementation.
8. **Members of the BWA Board, and BWA staff**, require continued training and awareness of gender sensitivity especially so that they can recognize privileges and discrimination around gender.

The key recommendations emerging are the need to:

1. **Ensure the existence of an enabling legal environment** that amends the BWA legislation with a gender and vulnerability lens.
2. **Establish a Gender Unit**.
3. **Appoint a Project Manager who is skilled in gender sensitivity issues**;
4. **Appoint a Social and Gender Specialist to ensure that project activities are gender responsive**;
5. **Approve gender equality considerations as part of the BWA’s strategic plans and budgets**;
6. **Approve mechanisms for quality assurance in the procurement of products/services**;
7. **Adopt a gender-sensitive monitoring, evaluation, learning, and reporting framework with project activities like staffing, administration, and activity monitoring, evaluation, and reporting**.
8. **Collect, store, retrieve, and analyse gender-disaggregated empirical data for use in making critical decisions**.


---
**Page 132**
---

# Gender Mainstreaming Action Plan

The main impact of the gender mainstreaming action plan is to (i) increase the participation of women and LGBTQA+ in decision making and raise the awareness of men, women and vulnerable communities on gender issues to minimize discrimination against vulnerable groups including LGBTQA; and (ii) improve access to water services for residents, especially women, and children of Barbados in the areas of health, agriculture, and employment.

The following outputs and activities have been included in the project to advance these gender mainstreaming goals:

- **Output 3.1:** Improved capacities of water technical personnel to operate, maintain and monitor and implement wastewater collection and treatment systems.
    - Activity 4.1.1: Develop and provide specialized and customized training to support the operations and maintenance of wastewater collection and treatment facilities including photo-voltaic power generation systems.
    - Activity 4.1.2: Provide capacity building workshops and refresher courses (SOP) and Operational Manual that addresses the requirements of the upgrades, preventative maintenance, operator safety, and environmental monitoring, including risks posed by climate change and gender and social inequalities.
- **Output 4.1:** Governance and planning roadmaps enhanced to enable use of reclaimed water in a controlled manner.
    - Activity 4.1.1: Undertake a legislative review to promote the Planning and Development Act, Wastewater Reuse Bill and other related legislations for enhancing wastewater effluent quality standards and promoting reuse applications. The review will also include recommendations for strengthening - private sector engagement, public-private partnerships, financial incentives, gender equality in access to water services, opportunities and access to males and females.
    - Activity 4.1.2: Develop a water resource and sanitation master plan that includes an optimal combination of decentralized, cluster and centralized water reclamation and reuse applications, with the centralized reclaimed water being transmitted and used for agricultural irrigation purposes. The development of this strategy will also take into consideration the social, gender-related and climate risks in the design and prioritization of water reuse strategies.


---
**Page 133**
---

# Output 4.2: Mechanisms developed/expanded to encourage the adoption of wastewater treatment and reuse applications by private individuals and businesses

- **Activity 4.2.1:** Develop a strategy and action plan to enhance the private sector in the provision and utilization of water, sanitation services, and the utilization of wastewater as by-products such as activated sludge. This includes conducting an assessment to identify opportunities for public-private partnerships in the water and wastewater sector, especially for the expansion of decentralized on-site wastewater systems.

- **Activity 4.2.2:** Develop a strategy and action plan for the expanded RAFF to promote gender equality and women empowerment

- **Activity 4.2.3:** Develop a strategy and action plan with recommendations for a gender sensitive and socially inclusive incentive programme to encourage conservation, recycle, re-use.

- **Activity 4.2.4:** Develop a strategy and action plan for the expanded RAFF to promote the adoption of decentralized on-site wastewater systems. The management of the expanded RAFF will ensure to promote gender equality and social inclusion as a requirement.

# Output 4.3: Gender Sensitive Public Education and Awareness Campaign Implemented

- **Activity 4.3.1:** Implement a Gender Sensitive Public Education and Awareness Campaign for community and tourists (visitors) through workshops, videos, community town hall meetings, site tours (demonstration of the plant technology and by-product reuse) and communication materials to raise awareness on gender equality in the management of vulnerable communities, including with the Association of Women in Agriculture.

- **Activity 4.3.2:** Implement a Gender Sensitive Public Education and Awareness Campaign which is dedicated to transparent measures of reporting, knowledge products, identify link to the Technical Mechanism and provide update to all stakeholders on the project activities. This page will also include a sign language interpreter and a large print version of the sign language to encourage social inclusion from disabled population. The project’s webpage will also ensure to report project results disaggregated by gender, where possible.

## Implementation arrangements of the project that will promote gender mainstreaming

In addition to the gender sensitive results and activities flagged above, the following implementation strategies have been developed to ensure that gender equality is considered in all aspects and considerations in the day-to-day operations of the project:

1. The Project Management Team will engage with key stakeholders throughout the lifetime of the project that will aim to engage women’s groups and other vulnerable groupings (all Components).
2. CCCCC Procurement policies, which promote adherence to the Centre gender policy and action plan.
3. Monitoring of indicators will disaggregate information by sex, where possible
4. The Project Management Team will ensure that there is adequate quality control for the mainstreaming of gender in the results and activities identified above, as well as to support the analysis of data on gender equality. This will be achieved through the inclusion of gender planning and decision-making on gender mainstreaming, including the early identification of possible unplanned negative gender issues emerging because of the project’s activities.
5. The Project Management Team will ensure that there is a gender focal person representative from the Barbados Gender Bureau.
6. The Project Manager will present to the PSC a report on the Project’s Gender Action Plan annually.
7. All members of the Project Management Team will receive gender sensitivity training on an annual basis.

# Financial Management and Accounting

As an accredited GCF entity, CCCCC’s Financial Management, Accounting and Procurement Services are aligned with the standards required by the GCF. This proposal is intended to be used for financial management and accounting of projects agreed with the Donor. These are detailed in the CCCCC Financial Manual and summarized below.

It is prohibited to use an existing donor currency bank account for a new project is prohibited. The utilisation of an old or existing bank account for a new project is prohibited. The signatory of the new budget must be the Project Manager/Coordinator. The final disbursement of funds from the project takes place within 180 days of a final financial audit or the last disbursement if a financial audit is not undertaken within those 180 days.

Project budgets are prepared and agreed during the conceptualization and form part of the final proposal and project agreement between the CCCCC, an Executing Agency and a Donor. All project budgets are uploaded to the accounting software to form the budget baseline required for accounting and financial transactions. The Project Manager/Coordinator is responsible for ensuring that all project budgets are available in the accounting system. The Project Manager/Coordinator is responsible for the provision of detailed budgets to the Financial Administrator.

The financial year for the project is dependent on the stipulations within the Project Agreement including the following: (i) the budget baseline is prepared and agreed annually; or (ii) the budget baseline is prepared on a rolling basis (monthly, quarterly, and annually) as or otherwise required by the Project Manager/Coordinator or other relevant agreement. Financial reports include the following information to date in both currency and percentages:

- Commitments
- Expenditure
- Unspent funds
- Undisbursed
- Variance
- Counterpart (if applicable)

Project payments and disbursements must be aligned in accordance with the approved Project Agreement and related budget (or its most current amendment). All payments are initiated by the Project Manager/Coordinator or other relevant agreement. The Project Manager/Coordinator and clearly outline the following:

| Field | Description |
| --- | --- |
| The Name of the Project (as per Agreement) | The name and code of the budget line(s) and against which the payment(s) should be made |
| The amount to be paid | Approved signed supporting documents which verify delivery or acceptance of the item to be paid (such as received deliverable, and signed deliverable slip) |
| CCCCC financial records are audited annually by an independent accounting firm, which is selected using a competitive tendering process. The audit includes review of all project accounts and provides assistance to the independent auditors in the following areas of planning, involvement, interim procedures as well as provide schedules, documents and information requested by the auditors in a timely manner. Upon receipt of the audited financial statements, the Project Manager/Coordinator reviews the CCCCC reviewer’s responses in writing to all management letter or other internal control and compliance report findings. The Project Manager/Coordinator is responsible for ensuring that all comments and accompanying reports are subject to review and scrutiny by members of the broad of directors who form the Finance Committee.

AML/CFT

Belize has in place a sophisticated legal, regulatory and institutional framework to ensure compliance with Financial Action Task Force (FAT) regulations. The Anti-Terrorism Act of 2002 is the main legal instrument in this context. Barbados is a participant in the FATF as well as its regional body the Caribbean Financial Action Task Force (CFATF). Belize has also signed up to the Inter-American Convention for the implementation of AML/CFT regulations, making available guidelines for specific target groups and sectors.

The CCCCC is committed in full compliance with international standards, the Money Laundering and Terrorism (Prevention) Act, Chapter 104 of Belize and any other applicable legislation in the territories of its Member States. The CCCCC has a strong commitment to the prevention and control of money laundering and terrorist financing. The CCCCC identifies, seeks out and accepts funding ONLY from established reputable and legitimate sources, which themselves have pledged strict compliance with the standard international legal framework governing prevention of ML and TF. The institution upholds the Rule of Law to
