GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 130

ensure that yours and that of its partners and beneficiaries is free from criminal interference and that resources are used for their intended purpose.

The CCCCG, by its inherent context, mandate and corresponding geographic location has to be ever vigilant in ensuring that the funds under its control are not diverted to any other purpose. The good stewardship of CARICOM Member States includes the enforcement components, land use, free trade, cash culture and offshore financial industry. Recognizing these predispositions is the first step by the CCCCG in instituting measures to ensure that its business apparatus is transparent, distinguishable and trustworthy.

The CCCCG does not associate with terrorist organizations nor distribute or allocate funds to partners who serve as fronts for money laundering or other criminal activities. The CCCCG also ensures that its own business machinery being exploited for the purpose of money laundering and/or our donor funds being diverted to terrorist activity is absent.

The CCCCG, in addition to supporting the Member States of CARICOM in coordinating Climate Change response. While the Centre's focus is on Climate Change as per its mandate, it can afford to protect the climate system and ignore the corrosive effect of ML and TF on the social and political institutions of the CARICOM region.

The CCCCG has established a robust procurement and accounting procedures that are designed to ensuring that its business apparatus is transparent, distinguishable and trustworthy. All the business processes of the CCCCG are therefore operated upon the principles of economy, efficiency, integrity and transparency.

In this regard, the CCCCG has instituted robust procurement and accounting procedures that ensure:

(i) A system to conduct due diligence on new clients
(ii) measures in place to prevent money laundering and financial transactions.
(iii) appropriate controls are in place to ensure that funds are fully accounted for and are spent in a manner that is consistent with the purpose and objectives of grant agreement and/or the CCCCG's mandate.

(iv) annual internal and external audits are performed and comprehensive financial statements are prepared, including an audit report by an independent auditor to be submitted to the Director General.
(v) compliance with the accountability measures required by its significant donors.

As part of its transparency framework, the CCCCG also maintains information on the purpose and objectives of the funding proposal. This information is available from the Technical Secretariat or the Director of the Technical Secretariat. This information is available directly from the Technical Secretariat and is accessible to the public on its website.

(a) The government of Barbados has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and policies; and
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is consistent with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for accounting no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP192

## Proposal name: The R3 (Reduce, Reuse and Recycle) for Climate Resilience Wastewater Systems in Barbados (JR-GbWWS)

### Accredited entity: Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre (CCCCC)
### Country/ies: Barbados
### Project/programme size: Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

   **Strengths**

   - The project design addresses the identified financial, social, technological and regulatory barriers to scaling up wastewater treatment in a country facing climate change.
   - It takes up wastewater treatment to diversify water sources and increase water security while using water in a circular economy mode using best practices and technologies.

   **Points of caution**

   - Social acceptance towards wastewater and reuse is a major concern, so implementation of component 4 would be key.


2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in annexure XIII titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. The impact of climate change is already evident in Barbados, Barbados is experiencing "absolute water scarcity." Approximately 310 m³ of water is available per person per year.


---
**Page 145**
---

# Green Climate Fund

**GEF/GCF.14/02/AMR03**

Page 157

## Placing Barbados Amongst the Most Water Scarce Countries in the World and This Problem is Likely to Become Worse due to Climate Change

### (a) Average Temperatures are Anticipated to Rise by about 1.8°C by 2041-2060 under the SSP2-4.5 Scenarios, with Daily Minimum Temperatures Increasing by 1.3°C

The number of consecutive hot days (i.e., heatwaves) has already increased and is likely to continue to do so.

### (b) Annual Mean Precipitation Is Projected to Decrease by About 9%, Increasing the Dry Season by Two Months and Impacting Crops’ Rate of Soil and Groundwater Recharge and Salinity

Water availability is essential to support the economy, and the climate crisis is already impacting water resources in Barbados. The frequency and intensity of droughts are increasingly aggravated. To counter this, Barbados is seeking to increase food production as an adaptation measure by 2035 while ensuring that food is produced without making water accessible under current and future water scarcity constraints.

### (c) At Present, Large Volumes of Precious Water Resources Are Going to Waste Because of Disposal in the Ocean Through Sewage Treatment Plants. The Country Must Change and Must Use Change. In the Collection and Treatment of Wastewater Is Necessary to Provide the Infrastructure for a Circular Economy Model Where Water Can Be Reused for Productive Purposes

The circular economy maximizes the use of scarce resources and minimizes the necessity of new resource inputs through the creation of an enabling environment where the basic infrastructure is put in place to provide the foundation on which the circular economy can take off. The project will focus on four outcomes: (1) the development of a circular economy model (hardware, software use) and through the creation of an enabling environment. The proposed activities are likely to be an effective adaptive response to the risk.

### (d) The Project Will Contribute to the Achievement of the Environment of the Health, Well-being and Productivity of Barbados by Using Carbon Neutral and Climate-Resilient Water and Energy Management Systems. These Systems Will Be Designed to Ensure that Water Is Collected, Recycled, Reused and Conserved. This Will be Achieved Through Four Outcomes and Ten Output-Level Results, as Described in More Detail in Section 2 of this Assessment.

### (e) The Project Activities Are Focused on Infrastructure Works and Transfer of Technology

#### (i) Specifically, Outcome 1 Aims to Achieve a Climate-Resilient Pathway through the Support of the Development of an Integrated Water Management System and the Installation of Decentralized Wastewater Treatment Systems in Two Zones. This Will Be Accomplished Through the Construction of a 20-km Pipeline for Aquifer Recharge and Agricultural Irrigation Through the Installation of a 9-km Pipelines Network for Rainwater Harvesting and the Development of an Urban Reclamation Process through the Installation of Decision Support Tools and Infrastructure;

#### (ii) Outcome 2 Will Span the Installation of Renewable Photovoltaic (PV) Energy Systems, Automated Controls such as Variable Frequency Drives, and a Sludge Dewatering System at the Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant. Currently, 0.3 MW of Solar Power Is in Place at the Bridgetown STP. An Additional 0.8 MW is Proposed, Delivering 13MW of Renewable Energy;

### (iii) Outcomes 3 and 4 Are "Soft" Developmental Measures That Primarily Aim to:


---
**Page 146**
---

(i) Create an enabling environment for achieving a paradigm shift in the approaches used for managing water resources (specifically wastewater), and thereby enhancing water security in the face of a changing climate. Specifically, outcome 3 aims to establish a new Water Resources Authority (WRA), for managing, monitoring and operating the upgraded system; providing customer service; developing and implementing operating procedures and the operational manual; and implementing a risk management framework.

(ii) Outcome 4 further strengthens the enabling environment through a legislative review process, including the development of a national policy for the adoption of wastewater technologies and reuse options; having nation-wide public awareness campaigns on the link between climate change and water availability; wastewater as a resource; wastewater treatment and reuse options; and promoting transparency and lessons emerging from this project; and

(iii) Outcome 5 aims to strengthen the enabling environment in the social, economic, adaptation, mitigation and environmental sectors.

8. The project is expected to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by 40 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over a period of 20 years following completion and full-scale operation. It will directly benefit 136,220 inhabitants (almost half of the total population of Barbados) by provision of accessible water.

9. The total project cost is around USD 50 million with a request to GCF for finance USD 39.4 million (78% per cent) in the form of grant. The remaining cost will be covered by BWA. It is worth noting that the total project cost has increased from USD 26.1 million for the Bridgetown and South Coast System to USD 183 in the short term, rising to USD 251 million in the medium-term scenario. This increase of 70% in the short term and 97% in the long term, falling to 16% in the medium term. The economic and efficiency analysis and assessment found that the grant provided by GCF is the only case where the project is profitable regardless of the time horizon.

10. The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is Category II, and the accreditation for the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) is part of the funding proposal package. Disclosure on the AE’s website is also completed in accordance with the Information Disclosure Policy of the Fund.


### Component-by-component analysis

#### Component 1: Wastewater reclamation and reuse (total cost USD 38.54 million; GCF cost USD 29.27 million)

(i) Installing centralized and decentralized cluster wastewater collection and treatment systems to achieve tertiary water quality for multiple non-potable water reuse applications that will increase water availability and the water sector’s resilience to climate change.

(ii) Installing conventional activated sludge biological treatment process;

(iii) Installing a run-off membrane filtration system;

(iv) Constructing a 9 km pipeline for irrigation and aquifer recharge;

(v) Implementing water quality monitoring programme (involving flow measurement and rain gauging equipment).

* There are three levels of water treatment: primary removes heavier solids; secondary degrades the remaining biological contents in the water; and tertiary removes pathogens and results in potable (drinkable) water.


---
**Page 147**
---

### Establishing an on-site laboratory.

#### Implementing the computerized real-time management system and:
- Constructing two decentralized treatment facilities in Zone A—Belle Vue and Belle Tenantry.
This component aims to enhance availability, management and use of tertiary-level reclaimed water to improve the water sector's resilience to climate change. Upgrading the Bridgetown STP to treat wastewater to a tertiary quality standard, including the two on-site decentralized treatment facilities, will reduce the volume of raw sewage discharged into the extraction of groundwater and thus reducing aquifer saltwater intrusion; increasing availability of potable water for agriculture and other purposes; improving the resilience of the island against climate change impacts on water resources for the island. In addition, it will increase water availability for agricultural/irrigation purposes and support irrigation developments in the Codrington area, which is currently limited by groundwater extraction to ensure food security. It will provide benefits to 155 farmers (60% males and 40% females).

#### Implementing Component 2: Decentralized Wastewater Treatment Systems
It should be seen as the first phase of a larger enterprise to extend and expand the use of treated reclaimed wastewater for irrigation purposes on lands along the west coast of Barbados and up to the public water supply system. The decentralized treatment systems will be constructed where feasible by the installation of reverse osmosis to ensure the appropriate water quality for irrigation purposes. This component is expected to provide a significant amount of potable water for transferring the water for agricultural development and augmenting the groundwater system.

#### Component 1: Will upgrade and install wastewater treatment technologies that are energy efficient (mitigation benefits), but it will also implement several decision support tools to help improve the resilience of the water sector against climate change, including operational hazards such as rainfall, including severe storms. These decision support tools will optimize the prevention of contamination associated with waste-water disposal and reduce operational hazards. The decentralized package collection and treatment systems that will be constructed in two communities (Belle Vue and Belle Tenantry) are expected to serve approximately 225 households, and is considered to be a "good demonstration" of strengthening the adaptive capacities of individuals and businesses to climate risks associated with water availability (adaptation benefits). The decentralized package collection and treatment systems will enhance collection and treatment systems that will produce reclaimed water suitable for agricultural irrigation purposes. This output will also expand the number of beneficiaries served by the current wastewater systems being used. This output will also expand the number of beneficiaries served by the current wastewater systems being used. This output will also expand the number of beneficiaries served by the current wastewater systems being used. This output will also expand the number of beneficiaries served by the current wastewater systems being used.

#### The project will be implemented in two phases:
- Phase 1: Upgrading and installing tertiary-level reclaimed water treatment and the water quality monitoring system by establishing on-site laboratory facilities will inform operations control strategies, improve decision-making processes for operation and maintenance, and inform decision-making and climate-resident building. Finally, the proposal addresses a number of Sustainable Development Goals (e.g., SDGs 2 and 1).
- Phase 2: Upgrading the Bridgetown STP to tertiary level treatment, which is principally applied to the upgrades associated with treatment plant (67 per cent). The source of the funding in GCF in the amount of USD 29.2 million [grant] and BWA in the amount of USD 9.8 million.


---
**Page 148**

# Component 2: Renewable energy and energy efficiency in wastewater treatment (total cost: USD 1.26 million; GCF cost: USD 1.26 million)

## Sub-component 2a: Focuses on enhancing the climate resiliency and carbon neutrality of the Bridgetown STP wastewater treatment system through the installation of renewable PV energy, and energy efficiency measures (total cost: USD 0.83 million; GCF cost: USD 0.83 million)

### Activities:
- Installing grid-tied PV renewable energy systems to offset increased power consumption associated with the centralized treatment plant process upgrades using category B1 equipment.
- Implementing automated controls and installing sludge dewatering technologies.

### Outcomes:
The outcomes of this component include achieving climate-resilient low carbon operations at Bridgetown STP, as described below:

(a) Installing grid-tied PV renewable energy systems to offset increased power consumption associated with the centralized treatment plant process upgrades using category B1 equipment. The upgraded system will be able to deliver off-grid energy for the upgraded Bridgetown STP, which will yield a GHG emission reduction of 1.635 Gg CO2 eq per year (or 0.8785 tCO2eq over 25 years).

(b) Implementing automated controls and energy efficiency measures (including variable frequency drives for pumps and motors) to reduce the overall energy footprint and reduce GHG emissions.

(c) By installing and implementing dewatering technology, biosolids will be captured and removed from the treatment process early, hence reducing the energy required to prevent sludge bulking. This would result in a reduction of GHG emissions associated with the treatment plant. The installation of the dewatering technology would result in savings on energy costs and sludge transportation costs, as well as environmental benefits including the reduction of GHG emissions and oil spills.

### Strategic plan:
A strategic plan is provided in the funding proposal to guide the replication of water treatment systems at other locations within Barbados. The plan includes the installation of a water supply by installing additional runoff treatment facilities along the west coast corridor, which would provide an alternative source of potable water for the island and could be used to supplement drinking water resources. This would support the project objectives enhancing the reliability of the water sector.

### Total cost:
The total cost of this sub-component is USD 3.3 million, which is principally applied to the procurement and installation of the 1 MW PV system and the sludge dewatering technology (B1 per unit).

## Sub-component 2b: Capabilities to operate, maintain, expand and monitor wastewater-related renewable energy technologies (total cost: USD 1.63 million; GCF cost: USD 1.63 million)

### Focus:
Component 3 focuses on enhancing options for scaling up wastewater reuse and enhancing the capacity of the Bridgetown STP to serve as a demonstration site for government and agencies, as applicable) to promote sustainability and the enabling environment for wastewater management through the development of standard operating procedures and an operations manual.

The additional USD 0.8 MW of power will be financed by the Government of Barbados (the host country is not counted in part of the co-financing for this project), albeit a Memorandum of Understanding is currently in advanced negotiations to secure financing for this additional 0.8 MW.


---

---

manual, flow and water-quality monitoring, and institutional human capacity development. In short:
(a) Update the standard operating procedures and the operations manual;
(b) Develop and implement a risk management framework at BWA; and
(c) Investigate and develop a strategic plan for the installation of run-off treatment facilities along the riverbanks.

22. This component will improve the technical capabilities of water technical personnel to operate, maintain and monitor, and implement climate change adaptation planning strategies for wastewater management in the BWA. It will also enhance the institutional capacity by increasing the knowledge of the BWA staff of the links between climate change and water availability and the impacts of climate change on water resources and water quality, as well as on human health effects.

23. This component will deliver training to approximately 400 individuals. In addition, it will investigate and develop a strategic plan for the installation of water treatment facilities along the riverbanks in order to reduce pollution from run-off into the BWA’s protected coastal ecosystems (see para. 19 above). This would support the objectives of this project of enhancing climate change resilience.

24. The outcome of this component is to have enhanced capacity and capability to support the preventative maintenance and climate resilience programmes and to ensure sustainability after implementation.

25. The total budget of this outcome is USD 1.63 million, which is principally applied to training and capacity-building and knowledge transfer activities. The outcome will be financed using IDA funds.

Component 4: Capacities (regulatory governance, awareness), buy-in and up-take within the private sector for wastewater management planning for the water sector (Total cost: USD 0.65 million; GCF cost: USD 1.46 million)

26. Component 4 focuses on enhancing the enabling environment for wastewater management in the BWA by increasing awareness of the importance of wastewater reclamation and of addressing climate change issues affecting the water sector and enabling policy and legislation. This component will achieve this through:

(a) Updating the existing regulatory framework to provide a road map;
(b) Developing a water and sanitation master plan;
(c) Developing private sector engagement strategies;
(d) Undertaking a review and providing recommendations on an incentive programme to encourage wastewater reuse; and
(e) Expanding the Revolving Adapting Funding Facility (RAFF) for the adoption of wastewater treatment technologies;

(f) Re-engaging with stakeholders and implementing public education and awareness campaigns; and
(g) Developing a project web-page and social media accounts to share progress on the project.

27. The outputs of this component are: (1) governance and planning frameworks updated/developed to enable wastewater reuse; (2) a funding mechanism for the adoption of water resources management technologies; and (3) an awareness campaign that includes a gender-sensitive public education and awareness campaign implemented.

28 The net outcome is that an enabling environment is created for wastewater technologies and use of reclaimed water. The enabling environment refers to the legislation, organizational structures and competency and strategies that need to be put in place. Achieving this outcome will require a high level of political commitment as well as a high level of partnership and professionalism that is needed to continually innovate and adapt to climate change, which in turn promotes sustainability for this project.

29 The enabling environment will be created through the design and viability of the project by providing mechanisms to encourage the adoption of wastewater treatment and reuse applications. In addition, the project will provide resources for addressing decentralized on-site wastewater systems are key to addressing inclusion and/or access challenges and ensuring that vulnerable groups can participate in climate change adaptation and/or mitigation initiatives. BWA, through the Water Sector Resilience Nexus for Sustainability in Barbados (WBN-Resilience) project established a fund to support small-scale water reuse projects. The WBN-Resilience funds are used to water sector adaptation and mitigation initiatives, including those identified as priorities by the Government of Barbados. The total cost of this outcome is USD 1.66 million. This was initially funded by the BWA and the Government of Barbados; however, IRECW will present a proposal for funding from GCF in the near future.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential Scale: N/A

As a cross-cutting aspect it is expected that the project will bring both adaptation and mitigation benefits, although with a focus on the former (over 90 per cent).

34 The project is expected to avoid emissions of 40,875 tCO2e over the 25-year lifespan of the 1 MW PV to be installed to offset the increased power consumption associated with the upgraded water treatment plant. This will be achieved by reducing energy consumption from using sludge and sludge dewatering technology, and energy-efficient pumps (with the detailed contribution of those to be determined after the detailed design is set during implementation).


---
**Page 151**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: N/A

The project will address the primary barriers to public and private investments in wastewater treatment in Barbados, including high capital cost, lack of expertise, unfamiliarity with technologies, and limited market for treated water. The project is expected to bring about a paradigm shift in the approaches to managing and diversifying water resources (specifically wastewater) and thereby the project will provide water security in the face of climate change. It serves as an example of how to increase public awareness and community participation and interest in the water sector, thereby scaling up the decentralized wastewater collection and treatment systems across Barbados and other vulnerable parts of the Caribbean region where water systems are becoming more vulnerable to climate threats.

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: N/A

In addition to achieving increased climate resilience for the water sector of Barbados and reducing GHG emissions by deploying renewable energy, the project brings about positive social, economic, environmental, and gender equality impacts that are aligned with six out of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (2, 5, 6, 9, 11, 13, 17).

The project will deliver environmental co-benefits through reducing the amount of wastewater discharged into coastal waters and rivers. This will improve water quality and improving the quality of soil for agriculture and other land-use activities. In terms of economics, providing GWP grants to farmers for the installation of rainwater harvesting systems is expected to promote construction and expansion of economic activities in the water consumption industry (e.g., tourism and agriculture). It is expected to increase private sector interest and participation in the adoption of climate-resilient technologies such as wastewater reuse systems and fertilizers (e.g. nutrients and fertilizers). The gender-sensitive approach taken by this project will ensure that considerations for women's livelihoods, particularly in the agricultural sector are taken into consideration throughout the project implementation. It will also result in increased participation of women in the production use and of reclaimed water.

3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: N/A

This project is designed to address the specific financial, social, institutional and technological barriers that have prevented the Government of Barbados so far from developing its wastewater sector by moving away from a linear "water" (or "waste") model to a circular economy model of water use. In recognition of the projected effects of climate change on water quality and quantity in Barbados, this project will provide technical assistance and capacity building for the Government of Barbados to develop wastewater management practices, implement new technologies that are climate resilient and sustainable, and increase the availability of groundwater aquifers for supply augmentation.

Barbados currently has a high debt-to-GDP ratio that stood at 144 per cent at the 2020 year end. This was further increased due to the Government's spending on the health sector to combat the prolonged impact of the COVID-19 pandemic. This indicates a very limited fiscal capacity for the Government of Barbados and therefore, there is not enough money available in the budget to cover the capital costs for this project and its national programme, through parallel finance.


---
**Page 152**
---

### Country ownership

The interventions proposed under this project are aligned with the country’s nationally determined contributions and relevant national plans, including the 2021 Physical Development Plan, the Barbados Growth and Development Strategy 2013–2020, the National Climate Policy, the Development of a Water Sector Strategy, and the National Adaptation Plan. These documents all stressed the importance of improving climate resilience in water security through diversifying the sources of water supply, increasing the efficiency of water use, and investing in wastewater treatments and its use for agricultural and supply augmentation.

The All, the Caribbean Climate Change Office (CCCCO) is a GCF direct access entity with a mandate to support countries in implementing climate change adaptation strategies. It has a proven track record in the planning, management and implementation of climate change projects and programs in the region, including in Barbados. The CCCC also has experience in supporting direct access entities through its direct access trust.

The proposal design is a result of collective efforts among stakeholders, including government agencies such as the Ministry of Agriculture and Natural Resources, the Ministry of Environment and National Beautification, the Ministry of Tourism and International Transport, the Water Authority of Barbados (WAB), private sector representatives, non-governmental organizations and affected communities. The project will continue to engage with these wider stakeholders throughout its implementation phase in order to ensure that their needs and concerns are addressed. The national designated authority also provided a letter of no objection and expressed its willingness to lead efforts for continued political buy-in and support at the highest levels of government.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

The mitigation cost is estimated at USD 98 per kW, which excludes the infrastructure costs associated with the installation of solar panels and high-efficiency energy and efficient pumps in the wastewater treatment plant. This mitigation cost is on the high side compared to other projects that have been implemented by the WAB over time; the small scale of PV installation, and the intensive capital investment into upgrading of the automated control system for the pump station.

The results of the financial analysis with and without GCF concession (50-50 grant/ high-concessional loans; 100 per cent grant) show that GCF providing in full grant is the only case where the project would be financially viable. The project would have to double its tariff level for irrigation/agricultural uses stands at USD 6.67/m³ so the project would have to double the figure to USD 13.34/m³. If the GCF provided a concessional loan of 50 per cent, increasing from 1.51 per cent in 2018 to almost 20 per cent in 2020, an increase in tariff would affect the level of economic activity by irrigating farmers with other effects on the local economy and food security.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### Project overview
The project aims to contribute to the enhancement of the health, well-being and productivity of Barbadians by using carbon neutral and climate-resilient water and energy technologies. It will be implemented through a combination of capturing, treated, recycled, reused and conserved. It involves upgrading the Bridgetown STP and installing cluster wastewater collection and treatment to achieve a tertiary water quality. The project will also install PV systems at the Bridgetown STP and at the proposed new Wastewater Treatment Plant (WTP) in Speightstown. At the Bridgetown STP, in addition, it will provide institutional capacity-building by developing standard operating procedures for the operation of the plant.

##### Environmental and social risk category
The project falls under Category B of the GCF environmental and social categorization system. The project is essentially an environmental mitigation measure with no significant adverse effects on human health or the environment, with only moderate environmental and social risks. The inherent risks of using recycled wastewater for aquifer recharge are mitigated by the use of tertiary quality water and the inclusion of design features including, particularly, the upgrading of wastewater treatment to achieve tertiary quality water standards. Other mitigation measures include the development of a reverse osmosis process and the application of additional measures to control for alkalinity and hardness prior to recharge.

##### Social impact assessment
The BfH has undertaken an environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and has prepared an environmental and social management plan (ESMP) for the project. The ESIA is a comprehensive study that assesses potential adverse effects on human health or the environment, as well as any other significant impacts of the project. The ESMP will be prepared for various aspects of the project during the engineering design phases, namely the installation of (a) photovoltaic (PV) systems; (b) decentralized wastewater treatment plants; (d) reverse osmosis process and (e) tertiary quality water standards. A draft ESIA condition is included in term sheet for the AE to deliver and disclose these studies.

##### Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards
ES1 (Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts). An ESIA was conducted on the basis of the general specifications from the project

# ESS2 (Labour and working conditions)

### ESS3 (Resource efficiency and pollution control)

The project is expected to improve energy efficiency through the use of reclaimed water for non-potable uses such as toilet flushing, cleaning, and irrigation. Water use efficiency is increased by recovering and recycling wastewater for non-potable uses, while energy efficiency is achieved through the use of low-flow fixtures, automated control systems, and a biogas digester to sludge and food wastes. River and marine pollution will be reduced due to decrease direct discharges of raw sewage and treated wastewater to these water bodies with the implementation of the project. The project is expected to reduce normal discharge scenarios, which occur 90 per cent of the time, only approximately 100m³ per day of reclaimed water being discharged into the river. This will not significantly impact present any significant faecal coliform and total suspended solids plumes in the sea.

The project is also expected to improve groundwater quality because the expansion of sewerage system will reduce direct discharges of raw sewage and treated wastewater to the aquifer. Moreover, emissions of odorous compounds, which had been the subject of complaints by communities living near the facility, are expected to be reduced due to the rehabilitation/improvement and proper maintenance of the facility's odour-control systems.

The ESIA provides a comprehensive environmental management plan, which includes the quality of infrastructure of the STPs, groundwater quality, river water quality, and ambient air quality.


# Community health, safety and security

Communities near the project will be exposed to construction-related safety hazards during the construction phase, including risks of accidents and exposure to harmful substances. During the operations phase, they may also be exposed to operational safety hazards such as fire or industrial accidents resulting in catastrophic release of untreated wastewater. The ESIA

The groundwater quality is expected to be positive given current contamination from pit latrines coupled with excessive water treatment plant. ### ESSS (Land acquisition and inventory expenditure)

Although the ESIA indicated that the project would not result in any significant displacement of people, new structures will be built on lands outside of current plant perimeter. The project will also include land acquisition for the following: the two new decentralized sewage collection and treatment plant systems, the reclaimed water pipelines, the injection well, pumping wells, and inclusion of solar PV power plants, among others. However, the project will comply with regulations and easements during construction that may entail lease or temporary possession of private lands. Lastly, the project will also include land acquisition for the following: the two new decentralized sewage collection and treatment plant systems in terms of permanent restrictions of access and/or changes or restriction in land use. Given that the project is located within a protected area, it will comply with compulsory land acquisition/fund use restriction, and/or displacement of dwellers/occupants. The term "displacement" refers to any action that results in significant change in the physical profiles of the lands to be used or affected by the project and a resettlement action plan in case acquisition of private properties or occupation of occupants cannot be avoided.

### ESSS (Environmental sensitivity and impacts on living natural resources)

The project's impacts on terrestrial biodiversity and natural habitat will be very minimal. The project will not result in any significant displacement of people, dwellers and city/urban areas. The current conditions of coral reefs around the project’s designated outlets have been assessed by the Barbados Coral Reef Monitoring Program as “poor” to “very poor”. Therefore, the potential impacts on marine biodiversity and natural habitat can be reduced.

#### a). Cultural heritage
According to the EIA, construction works for the reclaimed water pipeline (EWP) will take place within the limit of the UNESCO World Heritage Conservation Area's buffer and there is a listed building located close to the projected construction site. The area falls under the category of cultural heritage in the GCF Environmental and Social Management Policy. In the construction zone, one Cultural Heritage Conservation Area has been identified that contains buildings with historical significance. As per the EIA, the project will not affect these buildings; however, there is no indication whether and how these buildings will be affected by the construction or the operation of the EWP. Given that the route of the pipelines are yet to be finalized, it is recommended that the AE undertakes further studies on the potential environmental and social management measures to mitigate impacts on cultural heritage structures in project areas as part of the detailed design studies.

#### b). Stakeholder engagement
The ESMP provides details of the stakeholder consultations during the preparation of the EIA between December 2020 and December 2021. The consultations consisted of meetings, workshops, presentations and informal discussions with representatives from government agencies, environmental organizations and community groups, institutional representatives in the water sector and the general public. The ESMP also identifies the key stakeholders for each project phase. It identifies the various stakeholders, their key interests in relation to the project, methods of information sharing and communication channels that will be used during the project design and adjusted throughout the project implementation period. It is recommended that the AE discloses this information as part of the environmental and social management plan that will be prepared as part of the detailed design phase of the project.

#### c). Grievance redress mechanism
The ESMP outlines the mechanisms for feedback and adjustment to the EIA, including complaints/suggestions regarding the project. The project details have not been provided in the documentation. The mechanism is linked with the site inspections, monitoring and audits to confirm proper implementation of the ESMP, as well as effectively addressing any concerns raised by stakeholders during the project design phase and throughout its implementation.

#### d). Implementation arrangements
The ESMP provides detailed discussions of the institutional arrangements for the implementation of the environmental and social management measures. It includes a description of the roles and responsibilities of each institution, an analysis of the capacity building needs for the implementation of the ESMP as well as the responsibility for monitoring and reporting on its implementation.

#### e). Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment safeguarding
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision BIM-2021/18 requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment. The ESMP highlights section 154 of the Barbados Law on sexual offenses and further states that in cases where a project is implemented through a public sector entity, department or non-governmental organizations to ensure that the survivors are placed in a safe location and receive appropriate support services. In the short term, the AE is required to undertake or incorporate due diligence on SEAH as part of the environmental and social due diligence of the proposed project. It is recommended that the AE should conduct first level screening for sexual exploitation abuse and harassment (SEAH) of GCF financing adhere to the SEAH provisions of the GCF Revised Environmental and Social Policy.

# Gender Analysis

## Background Information

Barbados has ratified a number of international conventions. Among them are the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Platform for Action. The government has developed a national gender equality strategy to assist member countries with full implementation of the Beijing Declaration.

### Gender Analysis

The gender analysis provided by the AI gender analysis gives the following insights:

1. **Gender Representation**: Women are underrepresented in decision-making positions, particularly at higher levels.
2. **Employment and Leadership Roles**: Women hold fewer leadership roles compared to men, especially in science, technology, engineering, agriculture, and other sectors.

### Gender-Based Vulnerabilities

- **Water Management**: Women face greater risks from water scarcity due to their primary role as caregivers for households and communities.
- **Agriculture Sector**: Women are more likely to be affected by lack of access to irrigation systems, fertilizers, and other resources necessary for agricultural productivity. This affects their ability to support their families financially.

### Gender-Based Opportunities

The gender analysis also highlights opportunities for improving the lives of women in Barbados:

1. **Participation in Decision-Making**: Increasing women's participation in decision-making processes can lead to more equitable policies.
2. **Access to Resources and Services**: Providing equal access to resources such as water, land, and financial services can empower women economically.

### Conclusion

The gender analysis underscores the need for targeted interventions aimed at addressing gender disparities in Barbados. These include:

1. **Policy Reforms**: Implementing gender-sensitive policies that address specific challenges faced by women.
2. **Capacity Building**: Enhancing the capacity of women to participate effectively in decision-making processes and access resources.

## Risks

### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

The total financing is USD 49,900,000 of which USD 39,590,913 is GCF grant financed. Co-financing of USD 10,680,150 is a grant provided by BWA. Co-financing is at a ratio 1:27 and 21 per cent co-financing.

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (low risk)

CCCCC will act in its capacity as the AE, and also the executing entity (EE) with BWA as the co-executing entity for some of the activities. We view positively that CCCC has a track record of delivering projects on time and within budget. The project is being implemented under the implementation (PM06) Water Sector Resilience Nexus for Sustainability in Barbados (WSRN S- Barbados). BWA is also a co-executing entity for this financing, given its strong implementing capability in water and wastewater sector in Barbados.

### 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

Capacity-building activities under components 3 and 4 represent 7 per cent of the total GCF funding requested and may not result in realising long-term benefits as expected, in particular with respect to the sustainability of the project at end of implementation and sustainability beyond the project period. Execution delays could occur as capacity-building activities require significant time to be implemented.

Co-financing from BWA will be provided ultimately by the Government of Barbados through the Ministry of Finance, Economic Affairs and Investment (USD 10,680,150 in the form of a grant). The government has been under pressure to reduce its debt levels. This was reflected by Moody’s unchanged since July 2019) constrained by high debt levels and weak economic growth. However, the government has made progress on its fiscal consolidation efforts and delayed its ambitious reform agenda. However, relatively high income levels and low exposure to foreign exchange risk have provided some cushion against this risk. The Ministry of Finance, Economic Affairs and Investment, which has already sourced some of the co-financing from BWA, has also been able to improve its financial position significantly over time and we expect metrics to improve gradually over the next few years as the economy opens up and the Government rebuilds its primary surplus; and existing constraints are lifted.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

### CCOC as the AE and EE of the project
- BWA will co-execute the project with CCCC as the AE.
- The relevant GCF PCEs to be used for financing the project and guide implementation in accordance with its accreditation credentials, as well as guided by the terms of the funded activity agreement (FAA).
- CCCC, as the EE, will mainly provide technical support to BWA for the development of forms of tender documents, procurement equipment, goods and services.
- The GCF PCEs will be part of the evaluation team and report to the CCOC, guided by the terms set out in the Subsidiary Agreement, the procurement plan, and the terms of reference of the Government of Barbados.

### Project components
- The project components will be executed under the management office (PMO), while those under BWA will be executed under a dedicated project team.
- The project will be monitored by a project steering committee.

### Funding for the project
- Funding provided by GCF and BWA in the form of grants from the Government of Barbados through the Ministry of Finance, Economic Affairs and Investment.
- The CCOC will provide all major goods and services for the project using international competitive tendering procedures as outlined in the procurement plan. The CCOC, in accordance with its AFA, financial and accounting manual and procurement guidelines, will procure goods, works and services for the evaluation of the project outputs.

### Bank account
- Funding provided by GCF will be kept in a separate project bank account.
- The partners involved as co-executing entities are not obliged to report to the CCOC on the status of those activities. These entities are not obligated to transfer any monies from their own accounts into the project account or provide evidence of transactions and expenditures towards their co-financing obligations.

### Auditing
- The project will be audited annually by an independent accounting firm, which is to be selected using a competitive bidding process every five years.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP192

## Proposal name:
The R3s (Reduce, Reuse and Recycle) for Climate Resilience Wastewater Systems in Barbados [3R-CleWS]

### Accredited entity:
Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre (CCCC)

### Country/ies:
Barbados

### Project/programme size:
Small

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential
#### Scale: Medium to high

#### 1.1 Adaptation impact

##### 1.1.1 Barriers and opportunities

- **Barriers**:
    - The population density in Barbados is among the most densely populated and water-scarce countries in the world, with a population density of 600 people per km².
    - Water availability estimated at approximately 305–318 m³ per person per year.

- **Opportunities**:
    - The proposed project would increase water stress through the following impacts, expected for 2040–2060:

        (a) The mean annual daily maximum temperature would increase by about 1.1°C and the mean annual minimum temperature would increase by about 1.7°C.
        
        (b) The number of heatwaves has already increased over the period 1991-2020 and is expected to increase further with increasing temperatures; and
        
        (c) Annual rainfall in Barbados is projected to decrease by around 5 per cent, especially in the May–July season, extending the dry season by two months.

    - The decreasing amount and increasingly skewed pattern of precipitation and increasing evaporative demand due to rising temperature will increase water stress. This will further decrease underground recharge rates and increase salinization of aquifers, which provide a significant proportion of freshwater supply.
        
        A scientific body of studies suggests that sustainable aquifer yields could decrease by around 50 per cent by 2030. This is troubling for Barbados as the level of groundwater abstraction is already between 84 and 97 per cent.

- **The proposed project aims to enhance water-related climate resilience through the following actions**:

    (a) Upgrade of the existing Bridgetown Sewage Treatment Plant (BSTP) to achieve water quality for reuse: conversion to Bardenpho configuration, and installation of tertiary effluent treatment unit;
    
    (b) Construction of a distribution system and injection wells, to allow for irrigation reuse and aquifer recharge; and
    
    (c) Installation of two pilot treatment plants.


---
**Page 164**
---

# Installation of a 1 MW photovoltaic (PV) system at the BSTP to meet energy requirements of the upgrade.

(b) Implementation of energy efficiency measures and sludge dewatering at the BSTP; and

(c) Provision of technical and institutional capacity-building and support including, among other things:

(i) The development of a water resources management plan for Bridgetown that would include the replication of water treatment facilities;

(ii) A review of water-related legislation; the development of a water tariff structure to ensure financial sustainability in both the public and private sector; the expansion and promotion of the Revolving Adaptation Fund Facility (RAFF) to provide resources for the adoption of decentralized on-site wastewater systems.

### Direct beneficiaries are estimated at 136,000 people

Direct beneficiaries are estimated at 136,000 people, which is roughly half the population of Bridgetown. The BSTP serves approximately 25 per cent of the population who would benefit from the strengthened institutional capacity including, among other things: legislation review, development of a water and sanitation master plan and an action plan to engage the private sector in the provision of decentralized wastewater systems.

### The BSTP is a conventional activated sludge system

The BSTP is a conventional activated sludge system with a design capacity of 9,000 m³/day. In Bridgetown, there are approximately 12,500 properties connected to the sewer. The proposal estimated the flow based on water consumption, national average per capita consumption and number of properties connected to the sewer in Bridgetown. Only 5 per cent of the properties in Bridgetown are currently connected to the BSTP. Of those that are connected, only a small portion would be currently receiving an average dry weather flow of approximately 1,400 m³/day. On the other hand, the proposed upgrade is estimated at 23,000 m³/day. The current plant operations staff indicate is received by the plant, which indicated wastewater flows approach the plant capacity and that there are significant operational problems with the existing plant.

### For these reasons, a nominal flow of 9,000 m³/day was assumed to assess upgrading costs

In the opinion of the independent TAP, the BSTP is not operating at its design capacity (i.e., the pattern and concentrations of incoming pollutants) as the design stage is a major cause of plant failure and inefficient design, with potentially severe implications during the operation and maintenance phases.

### In a meeting with the independent TAP

In a meeting with the independent TAP, the proponent further clarified that subsequent to the preparation of the feasibility report, additional data has been collected from the BSTP. The data and samples have already been taken to characterize the raw influent and are being analyzed.

### In spite of the clear lack of knowledge on current average and peak flows

In spite of the clear lack of knowledge on current average and peak flows, the upgrade of the BSTP is proposed at a nominal flow rate of 9,000 m³/day. The proposal also includes an increase in capacity to 15,000 m³/day. The proposed upgrade includes two new aerobic reactors, two new secondary clarifiers and two new tertiary filters.

6. An assessment of wastewater flow and quality should have been carried out prior to investment decision making. The accredited entity indicates that "As there is no historical wastewater quality data available, the wastewater characteristics were estimated based on the existing literature." The proposed treatment process for the wastewater treatment plant is a biological nitrogen removal (BNS) process. Extensive experience in BNS wastewater treatment has been gained over many years and this technology is key for successful design. BNS success depends to a large extent on the influent flow characteristics, namely the peak flow rate and the daily average flow.

Every municipality has a distinct wastewater characteristic and to sampling campaigns should have been made to make a proper influent characterization.

7. In general, it would be necessary to conduct extensive omissions would be necessary for achieving reuse standards. But the high uncertainty in future inflow rates, and the very high treatment capacity proposed (15,000 m³/day) seems exaggerated based on the current situation.

# Scalability and replicability

To promote replication efforts, output 3.2 involves investigating and developing a strategic plan for the construction of domestic treatment facilities along the west coast corridor to collect beach groundwater and treat it as an alternative source supplementing drinking water resources.

The proposed decentralized wastewater system of the RAFF would provide resources for the adoption of decentralized on-site wastewater systems. The RAFF, currently being established by the BWA aims to mobilize funds for adaptation and mitigation initiatives in the water sector, including those described above, which are expected to be implemented at a scale that is replicable.

As described in the funding proposal, the estimated demand for reclaimed water for agriculture is 10.5 million gallons per day (MGD). The proposed project would meet this demand by this project, demonstrating the high potential for scalability and replicability of this project.

Institutional and technical strengthening would also support replication and scalability.

The independent TAP fully recognizes the urgency and importance of this funding proposal to address the water sector challenges in Barbados. It is not a usual approach applied in the wastewater treatment process, not considering methane capture and utilization as an additional energy source or flaring at the site. Therefore, the paradigm shift criterion is assessed as met.

# Sustainable development potential

## Environmental co-benefits

### Scale: High

#### 13.2.1 Environmental co-benefits include:

- GHG emission reduction from reduced sludge transportation (which is not calculated in the funding proposal and for this reason is considered as a co-benefit);
- Improved environmental conditions, including reduced groundwater pollution on the land where the treated effluent is sprayed, which is reportedly presenting "clogging problems" (saturated land);
- Reduced odor problems in the proximity of the BSPF from the enhanced sludge management;
- Reduced groundwater contamination at the sites benefiting from decentralized wastewater treatment systems; and
- The improvement in operation of the BSPF that would ensure higher efficient quality, and the reduced discharge flow to the ocean could potentially enhance water quality at the coast near the discharge point.

## Social co-benefits

The reduction in the consumption of irrigation water from natural sources would increase availability for domestic use, thus potentially enhancing the health standard of the population.


---
**Page 169**
---

### Economic co-benefits

1. The increased availability of water for irrigation and treated sludge for use as soil amendments has the potential to increase agricultural productivity, and consequently the income of the people who depend on agriculture.
2. It is estimated that the construction works included under this project at the SSTP and the TSTTP will create both temporary jobs and new opportunities for local businesses, which could have positive impacts on local income. Consultations related to baseline studies and strategic environmental assessments (SEAs) are ongoing.
3. The private sector engagement in the adoption of wastewater management technologies and use of the reclaimed water and other by-products would generate green jobs and have positive economic impacts.

#### Gender-sensitive development impact

1. Gender equality would be considered in the project activities as follows:
   - Stakeholder consultations throughout the lifetime of the project would aim to engage women's groups and other vulnerable groups;
   - Community engagement activities will include social and gender safeguards to ensure the protection of women, children and the vulnerable;
   - Community engagement would ensure that the needs of both female- and male-headed households are incorporated when installing decentralized wastewater treatment systems.
2. All training activities would aim to achieve a 50/50 balance in the participation of men and women.
3. The BWA staff would undergo a gender sensitivity training programme;
4. The update of the standard operating procedures (SOPs) and operational manual would include gender and age inclusion considerations;
5. The baseline studies would consider gender equality;
6. The water and sanitation master plan and the engagement strategy for the private sector would promote women's empowerment;
7. The monitoring of the expanded KEF would ensure that gender equality is promoted as a requirement.
8. The public education and awareness campaign would be designed to gender sensitive;
9. When possible, project results would be disaggregated by gender.

### Needs of the recipient

#### Vulnerability of the recipient and vulnerable groups

1. **Vulnerability of the recipient** Scale: High
2. The proposal includes a climate historical data analysis based on data for the period 1991-2018, with three stations located in Barbados (one of these having data on daily observations at the Caribbean Institute for Meteorology and Hydrology [CIMH]), and the other three was only monthly averages.


---
**Page 170**
---

## The historical trend analysis, based on the CMH data, was carried out using the Climat software and arrived at the following conclusions:

- (a) The minimum daily temperature has increased during the last three decades, at a pace of 0.15 °C per decade;
- (b) The maximum daily temperature has increased at a rate of 0.29 °C per decade;
- (c) There is no significant change in the number of small to medium precipitation events;
- (d) The total precipitation over consecutive days is increasing, especially during September to November; and
- (e) There were no significant changes in monthly precipitation during the period 1991–2020.

Additionally, the evaluation includes a visual trend analysis of temperature and precipitation, which is evaluated in the following paragraphs.

The temperature chart shows the increase in the lowest daily minimum temperature and the monthly average of the daily minimum. However, the trend is not gradual throughout the years but rather consists of three distinct hot summers. The summer of 2019 was somewhat higher median for the period 2010–2020.

The precipitation chart shows an increase in peak temperatures for 2006–2020 as compared to 1991–2005. However, rather than a trend, this is based on only three hotter summers during the period 2006–2020. If those three events are disregarded, there is no significant visual trend in maximum temperatures.

The proposal argues that the incidence of dry periods is increasing, with the two most severe dry periods (Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI) < -3) occurring in 2017 and 2019–2020. However, it can be seen that even though these two dry periods did occur in 2017 and 2019–2020, the trend of increasing precipitation for the period 1991–2015 suggests that there is no change in precipitation trends due to natural variability. The increase in SPI can be observed for the period 1991–2015. This suggests that even if there is a change in precipitation, it may not necessarily be attributed solely to climate change and anthropogenic climate change. These two severe dry periods can be regarded as anomalies.


---
**Page 171**
---

42. There is a weak statistical signal of an increase in the incidence of days with heavy rainfall (>100 mm per day). However, this trend appears to be statistically insignificant.

43. Figure 5 shows the results from the analysis of the historical data for phase one of the Coupled Model Intercomparison Project (CMIP6), and specifies which models were used. It should be noted that these models are not validated against historical data as proposed, and thus it can be assumed that the models were not validated against historical data.

44. In response to the questioning of the independent TAP, the proposer presented an analysis of the correlation between the average daily maximum temperature and the average annual precipitation, for the period 2015–2020. The chosen period is very short for validation purposes, but it shows that the models used in this study have a high degree of scatter (high dispersion), and demonstrate that projections significantly underestimate the historical data.

46. Also regarding projections, although the proposal states that historical data show that the average daily maximum temperature has remained relatively constant during the period 1901–2000, it predicts that it would increase by approximately +1°C on average for the period 2041–2060.

47. The models also predict that the average annual precipitation will decrease by 18 per cent for the period 2041–2060 compared to the historical data, and there is no indication of changes in average precipitation during the period 1991–2020. This can be observed in the SPI chart that shows a very low level of variability (low dispersion) from 2001 (2021–2060). This fact reveals the lack of validation of the models used to make forecasts.

48. The following charts, taken from the submitted document "C1 Climate and climate change in Barbados", show the evolution of the SPI index for Barbados using averaged daily observations of precipitation from a weather station in Barbados (up) and the SPI index for 1991–2020 (down). These data are aligned with the historical data used to develop the chart. The date axes are aligned to allow visual comparison. In the chart for 1991–2060 projected SPI, the data in the period 1991–2013 is a hindcast; CMIP6 forecasts start in 2014. In this case, it can


---

be seen that there is a clear correlation between the SPI based on observations and the SPI based in CRMPY results.

### Economic and social development

According to an Inter-American Development Bank survey, 17 per cent of the population of Barbados lives below the poverty line (15 per cent considered vulnerable). The current unemployment rate is estimated to be around 10 per cent. 

The significant decline in tourism due to the COVID-19 pandemic impacted the economy as a whole and particularly the agricultural sector, contracting the real economy by approximately 18 per cent during 2020 and 3 per cent in the first quarter of 2021.

During 2020 and 2021, Barbados faced other two crises: the eruption of the volcano La Soufrière in April 2021, which caused significant damage to infrastructure and the agricultural sector of Barbados, and the passage of Category 1 Hurricane Eta, which caused widespread flooding and damage to critical utilities such as power, internet and water; further reducing the productivity of key sectors.

### Absence of alternative sources of financing

Barbados's public debt-to-GDP ratio increased consistently from 55 per cent in 2008 to 144 per cent by the end of 2020.

### Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The need for institutional and technical strengthening is recognized in the proposal and addressed through a set of activities, which include providing training to all BWA staff concerning

# 1.5 Country ownership

## 1.5.1 Alignment with national climate strategy and policies

The proposal is fully aligned with the 2021 Public Development Plan and the Road to Barbados Policy Framework, which are both aimed at helping Barbados achieve its climate resilience goal and aim, in turn, through the Barbados National Climate Change Policy.

- The National Water Rescue Policy states that the traditional interpretation of the term "water resources" should be expanded to include, among others, reclaimed water and rainwater, as part of the total water resource.
- The installation of the PV systems is aligned with the National Energy Policy and Implementation Plan (NEPIP) goal to achieve a fossil fuel-free economy by 2030.

The funding proposal also contains a list of other relevant legislation and policies with which the present project is aligned.

## 1.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities or executing entities to deliver

The project would be co-executed by the Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre (CCCCC) and the National Environment and Wildlife Fund (NEWF). The accredited entity is the CCCC, a direct access entity.

The CCCCC is a regional non-profit organization among member States of the Caribbean Community (CARICOM), which provides policy advice and guidelines to the CARICOM member states and to the United Kingdom Caribbean Overseas Territories, and it is recognized by the United Nations as the focal point for climate change issues in the Caribbean. It is also the focal point for the Global Environment Facility Training and Resources Centre of Excellence.

The NEWF would assist with the implementation phase of this project and provide kind-in-kind support, particularly for those activities that require coordination among ministries and engagement with CCF for reporting and verification.

## 1.5.3 Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

The stakeholder engagement plan provides a list of key stakeholders consulted during the inception phase, which includes government and private agencies and non-governmental organizations.
- Agricultural associations and farmers
- The Association of Women in Agriculture

# 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium to high


---
**Page 174**
---

1.3.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

* The economic and financial analysis assumes a take price for water for irrigation reuse of USD $250/m³ (HBD $480/m³). There is no willingness-to-pay study regarding agricultural use.
* The feasibility study for the Provision of Reclaimed Water for Irrigation in the South states that the current pricing structure, which is based on a fixed take price and not willingness to pay could potentially reduce the long-term sustainability and economic efficiency of the project.

1.3.2 Amount of co-financing

* Considering the activities expressly included in the funding proposal, the co-financing ratio is quite low: ~0.2. GCF would be financing USD 39.4 million out of a total of USD 50 million.
* The capital expenditure, operational expenditure and revenues from the South Coast Sewage Treatment Plant are included in the economic and financial analyses presented as annexes. However, these revenues are not part of the funding proposal's budget. Therefore, when considering only the costs and revenues from the activities included in the funding proposal.

1.3.3 Financial viability

* Questioned by the independent TAP, the proponent answered that "... it was important to demonstrate that the project is financially viable". The TAP noted that the project is being financed by the Government of Barbados and the BWA. The SC-TCWRS, SCSTP and other development phases are not included in the economic analysis presented as annexes.
* It is requested for the proponent to paint a full picture of why grant funding, which is 29% of total planned medium investment, is being requested from the GCF. The TAP noted that the project is not part of the SCSTP. It is important for the Government of Barbados to take a loan to complete the proposed actions for the SCSTP. The grant, which is needed given the fiscal constraints of the country, is being requested in order to finance the development phases.
* Annex 3 covers the financial model for the programme but is dynamic enough to be used as a tool to switch off different development phases to cover the GCF's TAP.

1.3.4 Best practices

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Water availability in Barbados (estimated at approximately 305–310 m³ per person per year) makes Barbados a highly stressed country from a hydrological standpoint. Water stress is likely to increase as climate change progresses, and this project represents a sustainable measure to adapt to climate change and its impact on water resources. Additionally, the project will reduce GHG emissions by 25% compared with business-as-usual. Therefore, this project represents a paradigm shift for Barbados. This initiative has the potential to be replicable in other countries.

Thus, the independent TAP endorses this funding proposal with the following recommendations:

(a) To continue and, if needed, increase the effort in characterizing the influent wastewater (both flow rates and concentrations of pollutants), thereby reducing the risk of inefficient design and plant failure;

(b) To keep monitoring the actual plant operational conditions once the plant is commissioned. This information will be very valuable for future upgrades;

(c) To estimate the amount of emissions related to the proposed increase in wastewater treatment capacity (e.g., additional energy consumption at the sewage treatment plant, sludge treatment, etc.), methane emitted from increased amounts of sludge and other organic matter due to the nitrate-inhibition-decentralized within the Barbados process, and to re-evaluate these emissions and compare them to the GHG emission reductions claimed for the PV generation;

(d) To assess the potential for energy recovery from the sludge treatment plant;

(e) To look for private sector parties interested in installation of methane capture and cogeneration of energy within the facility (which would reduce GHG emissions both at Scope 1 and 2);

(f) To re-evaluate and make more realistic estimations of the operation and maintenance costs for the PV system.

## Impact potential

The AE is thankful for the ITAP’s overall rating of this criterion as medium to high for the impact potential. Specifically on the adaptation impact, the AE also thanks the ITAP for its consideration and recognition that the project design has been developed with a focus on climate change resilience during implementation. In addition, the AE also wishes to express thanks for the encouragement from the ITAP to further develop the project design in order to address the potential impacts of augmented influent characteristics, which will be characterized more intensely, to inform the detailed design and construction phase. The AE is also thankful that the ITAP has recognized the best available local and international technical knowledge and from regional experience to be the foundation for the project design. The AE further thanks the ITAP for its encouragement that the ITAP views the Project design as a step in the right direction towards the increasing climate change resilience of Barbados’ water resources. It is also encouraging that the ITAP views the Project design as a step in the right direction towards the increasing climate change resilience of Barbados’ water resources.

The AE thanks the ITAP for the recognition of the positive potential impacts on Barbados’ water security and quality through the implementation of the project, which will result from implementing higher flow rates to the plant and less pollution. The current NRW strategy being implemented by the BWI is multifaceted and takes a multi-step approach towards the reduction of its NRW, and the AE thanks the ITAP for recognizing that the Project design is in line with this strategy. The Government of Barbados through the BWI to reduce its NRW. With the Project design they are various opportunities to address the current challenges related to water security, water management: new network; wastewater treatment onsite, offsite and decentralized wastewater treatment; and the use of treated water for non-potable purposes, including agriculture. The AE thanks the ITAP for recognizing that the Project design is in line with this strategy.

The AE further thanks the ITAP for the recommendation provided through this project and highlights its response from the ITAP on the clarification questions. The Project components are all interconnected to provide solutions to the impact of climate change on the availability of potable water resources, including the use of treated wastewater for non-potable re-use of the treated water for cross-sectorial uses in agriculture. A critical element of the Project design model proposed by the AE is the decentralized wastewater treatment system which will reduce the uptake (thus increasing the availability of the already scarce potable water sources).

## Paradigm shift potential

The AE thanks the ITAP for the overall rating of high for the Paradigm Shift Potential of the proposed Project. The AE is also thankful for the comments received on the (a) Potential for knowledge and learning; (b) Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies; and (c) Contribution to the development of mechanisms to promote public accountability through public monitoring, which provide a roadmap to the interconnections of the various Project sub-activities across the Components. From the experience gained in previous projects, it is clear that the success of any paradigm shift campaign, contribution and engagement has been vital to any successful paradigm shift campaign and this also provides an opportunity for the AE to highlight how the Project can be used as a model of implementation as the various knowledge products are shared. Through the various mediums, such as videos, podcasts, webinars, workshops, seminars, conferences, publications, reports, newsletters, case studies, digital flyers, posters, 40 “water Wednesdays” (8 years) educational and current news programmes, and a 15-year archive of water-related media content, including social media accounts, serve as the various platforms to engage and to generate media literacy within the general Population.

With this Project considered holistically, i.e. aims, dimensions, and carriers, the willingness of its management to promote public accountability through public monitoring, which therefore constitutes a key element in the development of mechanisms to promote public accountability, leads the ITAP for the recognition of the measures that the Project seek to put in place as an effort to assist in the provision or capacity building of such mechanisms. These are to be developed to meet the paradigm shift in the water sector identified in (b) Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies, which is also identified in sub-activities (c) Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies, both with a major focus to catalyse Barbados’ action on protection implemented mechanisms to promote public accountability through public monitoring of water-related services. It is in this manner that the AE is thankful to the ITAP for the recognition of the vital importance of the Project’s contribution towards the development of such mechanisms as well as the development of frameworks to promote new water initiatives and water infrastructure systems.

Taking into account the sub-activities (b) Scalability and replicability, the AE recognizes that at a Caribbean scale, the Project can be considered as a model for other countries in the region to be utilized in both the scaling-up and replication of this Project, both globally and regionally where similar challenges exist. The AE is also thankful to the ITAP for the recognition of how the Project promotes a national PEF to provide opportunities at the grassroots to SMEs to become more competitive and sustainable. This is further supported by the fact that the Project can be much more than a business-as-usual and thus with the various Project components for the various knowledge products, it provides an opportunity for the AE to highlight how the Project can utilize additional resources, through resource recovery, on maximising the renewable energy (chemicals) that can be utilised. Once more, the AE is thankful to the ITAP for their recognition of how the proposed Project can be considered as a model beyond being labelled one off project or programme investment.
